#3089. A Stock Market Crash Could Be Life-Changing for Investors Who Have These 3 Things

Stock market crashes are inevitable, but missing the opportunities they create isn't.

A stock market crash can be frightening. Portfolio values plunge, economic forecasts worsen, and investors begin to question whether they should sell before things get worse. But for prepared investors, a crash can also create opportunities to build substantial wealth. Some of the world's best businesses can suddenly become available at prices that seemed impossible just months earlier.

The challenge is knowing what to buy and having the patience and financial resources to act.

Three things can make a significant difference:

The first requirement is a long investment horizon. If you need your money next year for a house deposit or other major expense, a market crash can be disastrous. You might be forced to sell investments at depressed prices.

But if you are investing money you won't need for the next 10 years, or longer, you have more flexibility to withstand temporary declines and allow businesses to compound their earnings.

Consider a company whose share price falls 40% during a market panic, even though its competitive position and long-term earnings prospects remain intact. An investor with a long time horizon can potentially benefit from the lower price. Someone who needs the money immediately may have no choice but to sell.

The key is to invest with money you can afford to leave untouched, rather than money you might need in the near future.

Imagine your favorite supermarket announcing a 30% discount. You would probably know which products you want before walking through the doors.

Investing should work the same way. Rather than scrambling to find stocks during a market crash, prepare a list of businesses you would love to own at the right price.

For example, Alphabet (GOOGL +0.78%) (GOOG +0.78%) is an interesting company to watch. It owns Google Search, YouTube, and Google Cloud, while investing heavily in artificial intelligence (AI). These businesses give it several ways to benefit from the growing use of AI.

Another example is Amazon (AMZN -0.35%). Its e-commerce operations, cloud computing business, and advertising division provide multiple sources of growth. AWS also gives Amazon exposure to rising demand for AI computing.

Both companies have substantial businesses and long-term growth opportunities. But that doesn't mean their shares are attractive at every valuation.

This is where preparation matters. Study the companies, understand their competitive advantages, and identify the risks. Then estimate their value and determine the prices at which you would be comfortable buying.

The goal isn't to buy whatever falls the most. It's to buy great businesses when fear creates attractive valuations.

Having the right time horizon and a shopping list won't help much if you don't have the money available when opportunities arise.

That is why investors should consider setting aside some capital specifically for market corrections. When markets fall sharply, investors with available cash have the flexibility to buy businesses they previously considered too expensive.

However, holding cash has a cost. Markets can continue rising for years, and nobody knows when the next crash will arrive.

One practical approach is to build an investment reserve and deploy it gradually. For example, you could invest part of your available capital when a company reaches your target buying price, then keep some aside in case its shares fall further.

Importantly, this money should be separate from emergency savings and funds needed for near-term expenses. You don't want to be forced to sell investments during a downturn because you need cash to pay your bills.

A market crash doesn't automatically create wealth. It creates opportunities, but only prepared investors are positioned to take advantage of them.

A long time horizon gives you patience. A shopping list gives you direction. Capital set aside gives you the ability to act. You need all three.

In other words, the next market crash could be one of your best investment opportunities. Make sure you're prepared before it arrives.

Lawrence Nga has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet and Amazon. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3088. What Does a ‘Blue Wave’ Mean for Mortgage Rates?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Democrats won big in the midterms, but mortgage rates have bigger forces to reckon with.

Mortgage rates fell again this week, extending a gradual slide that began well before Election Day.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage fell 13 basis points to 7.05% APR in the week ending Nov. 5, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

Homebuyers’ budgets are feeling the pinch from high mortgage rates, steep home prices and a shortage of listings. On the campaign trail, candidates from both parties promised to make homeownership more affordable. But solving that problem is like untangling 20 years’ worth of Christmas lights from your attic. Yank on one strand, and three more tighten.

“Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run and more housing supply plus a more dynamic labor market in the long run,” Jake Krimmel, senior economist for Realtor.com, said in an email.

A shift in political power won’t erase those challenges overnight. Here's what's driving mortgage rates right now — and where a Democratic Congress might have some pull.

How that affects mortgage rates: The relief of one less uncertainty is giving rates some breathing room.

Election results have cleared up one big unknown for investors. With Democrats controlling Congress and Republicans holding the White House, markets are bracing for the reality that any major spending bills could face roadblocks.

Political gridlock isn't always popular, but weirdly, investors sometimes welcome it. When a divided government limits new spending, the government may need to borrow less than expected — which could turn down the heat on mortgage rates.

But there’s a catch. That same gridlock could make it harder to pass laws to help bring down housing costs.

How that affects mortgage rates: Heavy borrowing from the government and tech companies adds upward pressure to the bond market, where mortgage rates take their cues.

When the government spends more than it collects in taxes, it borrows the difference by selling Treasury bonds. When there's lots of debt for sale, investors may demand higher returns, called yields. Mortgage rates tend to follow the yield on the 10-year Treasury note.

The new Congress still faces a mountain of government debt. What they choose to do about it can ripple through the economy to affect your monthly mortgage payment.

“Sound fiscal policy, rather than deficit-financed spending and standoffs over budgets and the debt limit, can help keep rates in check,” Andy Winkler, managing director for housing and infrastructure policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said in an email.

Meanwhile, Big Tech companies are borrowing a boatload of money to build AI data centers. To get the cash, they issue corporate bonds, giving investors another place to put their money. More bonds flooding the market can push Treasury yields higher, taking mortgage rates along for the ride.

But how long can the AI building boom last? Voters across party lines pushed back against data centers in the midterms. Even if lawmakers pump the brakes on new construction, a slowdown in tech spending doesn't guarantee cheaper mortgages.

How that affects mortgage rates: Wartime disruptions to global oil supplies can fuel inflation, pushing borrowing costs higher.

The unpopular war in Iran likely added to the voter frustration that helped fuel Democrats' midterm victories. Higher energy costs can hit household budgets in multiple ways: at the pump, through pricier goods that need to be shipped, and through higher interest rates on loans (including mortgages) as inflation heats up.

“A resolution would likely improve oil supply, helping to ease price pressures and potentially providing relief for mortgage rates,” Krimmel said.

A Democratic Congress could push for a different approach to ending the war, but it can't make Iran agree to a deal.

Meanwhile, stubborn inflation from the Iran war has raised the prospect of another Federal Reserve rate hike at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. Futures traders now put the odds of a quarter-point increase at more than 80%, according to CME FedWatch. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but investors anticipate its decisions — so mortgage rates often move before the Fed does.

If buying a home makes sense for your budget now, there's little reason to wait just because Congress changed hands. Buyers can sometimes score lower prices in winter, when sellers are more willing to negotiate. But with today’s mortgage rates hovering above 7%, higher borrowing costs can eat into those savings, so it’s important to compare mortgage lenders to get the best rate.

For longer-term hope, look to the bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which became law this summer. This legislation shows lawmakers can, in fact, work together to make meaningful progress on housing affordability.

“Even with divided government, lawmakers and the administration can build on that common ground to further boost housing production, preserve existing affordable homes, and help families struggling with high housing and utility costs,” Winkler said.

But new homes take time to build, and your house hunt doesn't have to follow Congress's calendar. Your homebuying budget and local market conditions matter more than the next political headline.

#3086. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

#3085. Marriott Bonvoy Boundless vs. Bevy: Which One Comes Out On Top?

The right card for you may depend on where you travel, how often, and your level of elite status with Bonvoy.

Two of the popular credit cards in the Marriott Bonvoy portfolio deliver various benefits to their users. The right one for you may depend on your typical travel patterns and history, such as if you already have a high level of elite status with Marriott or you travel only to certain regions.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card both come with valuable perks, but there are quite a few considerations that are worth a thorough review. Many travelers prefer not to have two cards from the same loyalty program, and these two cards are actually quite different from each other (including being issued by different banks).

Let’s take a look at these two Marriott co-branded cards to see if one may serve your needs over the other.

Here's how the cards stack up on the basics.

Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card

While the “winning” card for some may not apply to everyone, we wanted to analyze the card that provides exceptional benefits for travelers looking for a Marriott card in exchange for the cost of the annual fee. Those new to Marriott will find this to be a solid entry-level card, but it also works for Marriott Bonvoy fans looking for some extra perks without a huge outlay of money for an annual fee. These are the benefits that handed this card the win.

The annual fee on the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is notably less at $95 compared with $250 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card. While the earning rates when using the card to pay for Marriott stays are nearly the same, and both have bonus categories where you can earn multiples of points based on everyday spending, the lower-annual-fee card offers a lot of points earning for its low annual fee.

On top of the lower annual fee, it comes with an automatic annual free night award, Silver Elite status and a fast path to achieve Gold if you use the card often. Add to that an annual $100 in airline credits and DoorDash DashPass membership, and you have more than covered the annual fee without considering the other perks.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card’s $250 annual fee is higher, and its annual free night award requires spending $15,000 in eligible purchases. But the card comes with automatic Gold Elite status and more bonus points earned.

There are other cards (like American Express Platinum Card®) that also include free Gold Elite status with Marriott that can provide decent value for their annual fee (enrollment required). From a pure cash maximization perspective, the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card wins because it provides more bang for your buck.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is the only one of the two cards to include an annual free night award, and it has a lower annual fee. It can be used at properties up to 35,000 points a night. This can render great value and is more than worth the $95 annual fee. Hands down, it is the winner in this category.

If you spend more than $15,000 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card in a calendar year, you can unlock a free night certificate valid at up to 50,000 points. This is even more valuable than the included certificate that comes with the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, but it also requires you to spend more on this card to get it.

It is important to consider the opportunity cost of not putting certain types of spend on other cards where you could be earning greater multiples of points for the same type of spending. Would losing those points be worth the value of a one-night-free certificate? Since Marriott relies on dynamic pricing, which means that points rates for a redemption night can fluctuate regularly, in many cases the value may not reach the level of the missed points opportunity.

While both cards offer decent travel protection benefits, only the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card comes with a flight credit. You can earn up to $100 in statement credits when making purchases directly with airlines by spending $250 on the card before the end of 2026 for a $50 statement credit and another $50 credit by spending $50 before June 30, 2027. That gives another point to the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card Boundless card.

» Learn more: Should I be loyal to a single airline?

If you don’t already have a Marriott co-branded credit card and you want to diversify what’s in your wallet, this could be a great option for big spenders. And if you are looking for one of the fastest paths to high Marriott elite status, this card can help you get there.

Both the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card cards offer bonus points on spending, but the more expensive annual fee Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card offers more points on more spending opportunities, including the below:

18.5X points on Marriott Bonvoy property spending (1.5X more than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card card).

4X points at global restaurants, including U.S. takeout and delivery plus U.S. Supermarkets on up to $15,000 in combined purchases, then 2X points (that’s 1X more than what Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card offers and available on $9,000 more in spending).

1,000 bonus points when staying at a Marriott Bonvoy property.

Depending on how much you spend in these categories, you have the ability to earn more points with this card than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, including when you are not traveling. And then when you are, you earn 1,000 Marriott bonus points for each paid, eligible stay booked directly with Marriott. This gives it an edge, even though the annual fee is higher.

» Learn more: Hilton versus Marriott — Which one is right for you?

When you get the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, you’ll receive 15 elite night credits deposited into your Marriott account. This is on top of the instant Gold Elite status you earn with the card, which normally requires staying 25 nights. To reach the next tier, Platinum, you only need an additional 10 nights to reach its 50-night minimum.

This means not only do you start out at a higher elite tier with Marriott just for having this card, but also you can progress more quickly to Platinum. For frequent travelers, the best value of this card is the opportunity to reach the next status tier faster. And if you wanted to keep going, you would only need 35 more nights to get to Titanium, which is rich with on-property perks like lounge access, welcome gifts and room upgrades.

Earning these nights within a calendar year is not difficult for Marriott loyalists, especially if you hit the jackpot during a promotion that offers double elite nights.

The status perks don’t stop there. Once you hit Marriott’s Titanium status, you can then link your account with United Airlines to earn United’s Premier Silver elite status for more benefits when you fly United, including greater baggage allowance and free extra-legroom economy seats at check-in.

» Learn more: Marriott elite status: what you need to know

Both of these cards provide Marriott Bonvoy points earning and elite status, and they both offer a great way to expedite points earning or receive travel and purchase protections when using the card. But the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card packs a greater punch in its benefits for a lower annual fee. It becomes a great additional card to others you may have (like the American Express Platinum Card®, which has its own impressive travel perk lineup).

If you are a Marriott frequent guest, the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card offers higher-tier Gold status and a faster path to even better tiers for perks. But, it costs more each year and does not offer a free annual night.

Deciding which perks are more important for you, as well as looking at what benefits come from other cards in your wallet, will help you make an informed decision on which of the two best suits your needs.

For rates and fees of the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, please visit American Express' rates and fees page.

#3084. Daqo: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Daqo is betting its future on two fronts: stabilizing polysilicon margins while building a new revenue stream in AI data center power.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Daqo (DQ +6.48%) announced a substantial increase in quarterly sales volume following a shift to market-oriented pricing. The strategic pivot helped narrow operating losses while the company maintained a debt-free balance sheet with $2.06 billion in liquid assets. For long-term investors, navigating severe solar sector headwinds requires balancing low-cost manufacturing efficiency with disciplined capital allocation.

Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Daqo abandoned its previous wait-and-see posture in June, choosing instead to align pricing with prevailing market dynamics. This shift helped third-quarter sales volume rebound to 46.9 thousand metric tons from 15.2 thousand metric tons in the prior quarter, narrowing operating losses despite soft solar industry conditions.

Elevated solar supply chain inventory of roughly 700,000 metric tons continues to weigh on selling prices across the market. In response, Daqo plans to lower its fourth-quarter production volume to between 35,000 metric tons to 45,000 metric tons, aligning plant output with actual customer demand to support broader market recovery.

To hedge against volatile solar market cycles, Daqo is expanding into power equipment for artificial intelligence data centers (AIDC), such as solid-state transformers and energy storage systems. Partnering with an affiliated parent company that brings four decades of manufacturing expertise, the company expects initial revenues from this segment in 2027.

Solar market demand is stabilizing as price floors take effect, and Daqo maintains strategic flexibility with $2.06 billion in cash assets and zero debt. Low production costs across its two facilities support long-term durability during cyclical industry troughs.

Management is evaluating potential stock repurchases while observing regulatory enforcement and peer output cuts. Operational focus remains on digital automation to optimize unit costs.

#3083. This Stock Is a Nearly Guaranteed Winner From the AI Infrastructure Buildout

Taiwan Semiconductor is a key supplier to almost every major logic chip designer.

Investing to profit from the AI build-out can be difficult. There are so many options, ranging from computing companies to application plays to energy companies. All of these are bound to benefit in one way, but choosing the best isn't easy. However, I think there's one company that may cut through all of that noise and provide a top investment opportunity: Taiwan Semiconductor (TSM +6.33%).

It looks like a practically guaranteed winner in the AI build-out, and it's easy to see why.

Taiwan Semiconductor is a logic chip fabrication company: Simply put, it manufactures the chips designed by its clients, most of which lack any foundries of their own. All of the major AI computing unit manufacturers -- Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%), AMD (AMD +11.04%), and Broadcom (AVGO +0.74%), and others -- utilize TSMC's services.

They have to, as no other chip foundry has the capacity to serve as a viable alternative. During Q2, TSMC's massive manufacturing infrastructure allowed it to take in 72.5% of chip foundry revenue in the world. That capacity dominance makes it difficult for any other company to rise and steal its leading clients away.

However, even if it were more of an option, TSMC's clients don't have many reasons to want to switch foundry partners, either. TSMC has best-in-class technology and a reputation for fantastic production yields. That makes it a great company to partner with. There's a reason why TSMC has grown to become the giant that it is.

As for the AI build-out, as long as companies continue to need more and more computing capacity, TSMC will see large orders for chips. It has projected that from 2024 to 2029, revenues from its AI chip business will grow at a compound annual rate in the mid-to-high 50% range, and during the company's Q3 conference call, CEO C.C. Wei said that its growth rate "probably will be higher."

That will benefit shareholders immensely.

Lastly, the stock isn't priced outrageously.

At 23 times forward expected earnings, Taiwan Semiconductor is trading at a discount relative to its typical level at this time of year. So, not only is it a great long-term investment in the AI build-out, but it's also a timely buy. Opportunities like this don't often appear, and investors should take advantage of the stock while it's a bit cheaper than historical levels. While TSMC may not provide the best long-term AI returns, I am confident that it is practically guaranteed to do well.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Xylem (XYL -2.22%) announced plans to sell lower-margin assets and shift to a three-segment structure. This strategic pivot sharpens the company's focus on faster-growing industrial markets with higher profitability. Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Management is pruning non-core businesses to focus capital on higher-margin industrial markets. Exiting these lines helps the company shed lower-margin revenue and streamline operations into three focused segments.

Macroeconomic pressures are forcing municipal water utilities to delay short-cycle replacement orders. Management expects orders to convert into sales at a slower pace while utilities navigate high interest rates and budget constraints.

Demand for specialized cooling and water treatment in artificial intelligence infrastructure is creating a strong growth engine. High-tech manufacturing wins are expanding the company's reach in attractive industrial sectors.

Demand for critical water infrastructure remains resilient even as near-term project conversion slows. The company's expanding backlog, pricing discipline, and operational productivity provide a strong foundation to navigate short-term delays.

Long-term value creation hinges on executing portfolio divestitures while capturing higher-margin industrial opportunities. Management's strategic realignment positions the business for accelerated growth.

#3081. 2027 Tax Brackets and Federal Income Tax Rates

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Each year, the IRS adjusts tax brackets to keep pace with inflation. The 2027 tax brackets were announced on Nov. 5, 2026.

The IRS has released updated tax brackets for 2027, which apply to the taxes we file in 2028. You may notice that the seven tax rates generally stay the same year-to-year — so why do tax brackets get adjusted?

Well, due to inflation, our money might not stretch as far as it did the previous year. To combat that, the IRS adjusts tax brackets so taxpayers can keep a little bit more of their money in a lower tax bracket compared with the previous year.

For example, a single filer could make up to $50,400 in 2026 and pay a top tax rate of 12%. But in 2027, they could make up to $52,025 and still only pay a top tax rate of 12% thanks to these inflation adjustments.

Not sure how to read the tax bracket tables below?

Find your total taxable income in the "tax bracket" column. The corresponding tax rate is the highest rate you'll pay — it doesn’t apply to all your income; it applies only to the portion that falls within that bracket. The income in the lower brackets is taxed at those lower rates.

For example, let's say you’re a single filer and you expect to earn $150,000 this year. The top rate you’d pay on a portion of your income is 24%. The rest would be taxed at 10%, 12% and 22%.

#3080. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

#3079. AppLovin Shares Surge as Revenue Continues to Soar. Is It Too Late to Buy the Stock?

AppLovin stock had been in the dumps, but this could be the start of a big rally.

Shares of AppLovin (APP +9.86%) spent much of this year in the doghouse as investors worried about rival Unity Software's (U +5.51%) progress. AppLovin missed second-quarter expectations after its model performance failed to improve at its usual pace. However, the stock surged after its third-quarter results zoomed past expectations.

Given the adtech company's growth and valuation, AppLovin still looks like a buy after this rebound. Let's dig into its results and prospects to see why the artificial intelligence (AI) stock still looks like a good buy.

AppLovin has been one of the biggest beneficiaries of using AI to drive growth in its core business, with the launch of its AI adtech platform, Axon 2.0, in 2023 being a game changer for the company.

This was on full display once again in the third quarter as revenue soared 55% to $2.18 billion. That topped the $2.07 billion analyst consensus, as compiled by Bloomberg.

The growth was driven by both improvements in its core gaming business and its start to gain momentum in the consumer industry. Its gaming business grew nearly 50%, fueled by the partial rollout of the transition to a new model.

It said the current model transition that it is undergoing is as meaningful as the launch of its AXON 2 platform and that it enhances performance in areas where there isn't as much data, such as with newer advertisers, smaller advertisers, and in newer categories like the consumer space. It said advertiser performance was strong in the quarter, with customers seeing higher profitability on their ad spending, leading to an increase in ad budgets.

Meanwhile, CEO Adam Foroughi said that any success competitors were seeing was not impacting its growth. He said that this is not a zero-sum game and that their improvements benefit the market as a whole.

AppLovin's newer consumer vertical saw revenue grow by triple digits year over year in the quarter. This business continues to add runway, and the company believes this vertical will help the overall company compound at 30% annually over the long term.

Adjusted earnings per share (EPS) also came in above expectations. EPS from continuing operations climbed 76% from $2.39 a year ago to $4.30, topping the consensus by $0.26. Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) climbed 59% year over year to $1.84 billion.

The company also continues to generate a ton of cash. In the quarter, it generated free cash flow of $1.4 billion. It ended the quarter with just over $340 million in net debt, down from $1 billion at the start of the year. Management also repurchased 4.1 million shares in the quarter, worth $1.4 billion, taking advantage of its weak stock price.

Looking ahead, AppLovin projected fourth-quarter revenue between $2.365 billion and $2.395 billion, representing growth of 43% to 44%. The guidance easily surpassed the $2.25 billion analyst consensus. It guided for adjusted EBITDA to be between $1.995 billion and $2.025 billion, representing growth of between 43% and 45%.

AppLovin showed that one quarter does not make a trend, with its results and guidance both coming in well above analyst expectations this time around after a rare second-quarter miss. The company continues to deliver robust growth in its core gaming ad segment, while the newer consumer segment shows promise. Meanwhile, it looks like the new model improvements have strong potential to keep growth climbing.

Despite the rebound in the stock, it remains cheap given its growth, with a forward price-to-earnings ratio below 18 times 2027 analyst estimates even after the huge surge in its price. As such, I don't think it's too late to buy it, with AppLovin still trading at a multiple well below where it has been in the past.

#3077. IRS Announces 2027 Income Tax Brackets, Updated Standard Deduction

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Here's a rundown of changes to some well-known tax provisions for the 2027 tax year.

Each year, the IRS updates common tax provisions, such as income tax brackets and the standard deduction, to ensure the tax code is keeping pace with the rising cost of living.

These tweaks — formally known as inflation adjustments — help prevent “bracket creep,” where taxpayers who received a cost-of-living raise can pay more in taxes even though their purchasing power remains unchanged.

Inflation adjustments for the 2027 tax year were set at 3.2% — larger than the previous two years as inflation heated up. This could mean lower tax bills for some people when they file in 2028.

In the U.S., there are seven marginal rates — 10%, 12%, 22%, 24%, 32%, 35% and 37% — which will remain unchanged for 2027.

Contrary to a popular myth, it’s uncommon for people to pay a single flat federal tax rate on their taxable income. Instead, portions of a person’s income can be taxed at different rates depending on which tax brackets they fall into. For example, some of a person’s income could be taxed at 10%, another chunk at 12%, and so on.

For 2027, the IRS is adjusting those tax brackets — the windows of income that dictate where a tax rate ends and begins — for each filing status. The adjustments mean that some people may be able to keep some of their income in lower tax brackets, and those who received a cost-of-living raise may avoid getting a portion of their income pushed into a higher bracket.

Married filers, for example, can make up to $104,050 to remain with a top tax rate of 12%, compared with $100,800 in 2026. This increase could allow some couples to shelter an additional $3,250 from a higher tax rate in 2027.

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Single filers

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Heads of household

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Married filing separately

» MORE: See the full 2027 tax brackets for each filing status

The IRS lets most filers lower their taxable income by taking the standard deduction or itemizing their returns. The standard deduction, a flat amount based on filing status and age, is popular, as it requires less work to claim and is often more beneficial for those who might not have deductible expenses.

For 2027, the standard deduction will be $16,600 for single filers and those married filing separately, a $500 increase from 2026. Joint filers will be eligible to take $33,200 and heads of household can take $24,925, a $1,000 and $775 increase over 2026, respectively.

» Need help deciding whether to itemize or take the standard deduction? Learn about the differences

Also included in the IRS' 32-page inflation release are various updates to several tax provisions beyond the federal tax brackets. Plus, a brand-new tax credit will go into effect in 2027.

Starting in 2027, taxpayers who contribute to a health flexible spending account (FSA) can contribute up to $3,500 and, if their plan permits, carry over up to $700 into the next tax year.

For those with health savings accounts, the 2027 limit for contributions will rise to $4,500 for self-only coverage and $9,000 for family coverage.

The annual exclusion for gifts, which limits how much taxpayers can give an individual without filing a gift tax return on certain gifts, will be $20,000 per person in 2027, an increase of $1,000 compared with 2026.

The lifetime estate tax exclusion establishes a threshold for the taxation of estates upon a person's death. In 2027, estates valued at or below $15.48 million will not be subject to estate tax, up from $15 million in 2026.

The Education Freedom Tax Credit will launch on Jan. 1. According to the IRS, it aims to support private contributions for K-12 scholarships. Taxpayers who donate to a "scholarship granting organization" in one of the 30 participating states can receive a nonrefundable tax credit of up to $1,700 ($3,400 for couples filing jointly).

#3074. Here's How Much $10,000 in Altria Stock Pays You Each Year

Changing smoking habits may not necessarily strip Altria of its dividend stalwart status.

Altria Group (MO -1.88%), parent company of Marlboro maker Philip Morris USA, has one of the highest forward yields among stocks in the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%). It has also raised its quarterly dividend annually for 57 consecutive years, placing it among the Dividend Kings, stocks with at least 50 years of consecutive dividend growth.

While this company's days of high growth are behind it, a path remains for the stock to sustain and keep on slightly growing its quarterly payouts in the years ahead. Dividend investors should still consider it a buy.

With Altria trading for around $78 per share, a $10,000 investment in the stock equates to around a 128-share position. Altria pays a $1.11-per-share quarterly dividend. Annualized, that comes out to $4.44 per share. Multiply that by 128 shares, and you get $568.32 in annual dividend income, giving it a 5.7% annual dividend yield. That's over 5 times the annual dividend yield of the S&P 500, which currently stands at around 1.1%.

Altria most recently declared a dividend increase in August, raising the quarterly payout by 4.7%. This is in line with management's current dividend growth policy, which targets "mid-single-digit dividend per share growth annually."

Yes, Altria's 88.5% payout ratio looks very high. However, with rising cigarette prices and growing sales of "smokeless" products, the company's dividend policy appears sustainable.

Altria recently raised its full-year earnings growth guidance to between 4.5% and 6%, bolstering confidence that the company can both maintain its dividend and keep raising it at a modest pace in the years ahead.

"Going smokeless" remains a greater challenge for Altria than the success competitors like Philip Morris International have had in this area, but as sales of its smokeless products continue to help augment overall growth, the stock appears well positioned to maintain its status as both a high-yield dividend stock and a Dividend King.

#3069. Why Centrus Energy Stock Crashed 26% in October

Centrus went shopping, and Wall Street isn't happy with its purchase.

Limping toward the end of 2026, Centrus Energy (LEU +17.49%) stock fell 18.6% in September and continued to decline last month. Analysts took a more bearish stance on the nuclear stock, and investors were clearly paying attention.

According to data provided by S&P Global Market Intelligence, Centrus Energy stock fell 26% in October, while the S&P 500 dipped 0.5%.

On Oct. 19, Centrus announced its acquisition of Westerman Holdings, which the company characterizes as one of its largest and most diversified suppliers. Centrus believes that the transaction, funded with about $95 million in cash and $21 million in equity, will contribute to its adjusted earnings per share in the first year following its closing.

Centrus lauded the acquisition as "a significant milestone ... as it builds supply chain resilience while diversifying its revenue base." Westerman provides Centrus to a variety of industries beyond nuclear, including oil and gas and other industrial sectors.

Investors immediately expressed their disapproval of the acquisition. Centrus shares dropped more than 4% on Oct. 19 from the stock's previous close.

On Oct. 20, analysts began to weigh in on the acquisition -- and the opinions were hardly encouraging. Roth Capital slashed its price target on Centrus to $115 from $188, maintaining a neutral rating. According to Thefly.com, the firm updated its model after Centrus acquired Westerman Holdings and believes that the company's third-quarter financial results could disappoint investors.

In the ensuing days, firms shared a variety of other downwardly revised price targets.

Centrus is a leading provider of nuclear fuel and services for the nuclear power industry. One area in which the company distinguishes itself is the production of high-assay low-enriched uranium (HALEU), a specialized form of nuclear fuel required by small modular reactors (SMRs), the next generation of nuclear reactors. At the end of September 2026, Centrus reported a backlog of $5.4 billion for both low-enriched uranium (LEU) and HALEU businesses.

While firms may have soured on Centrus stock due to its acquisition of Westerman, long-term investors should hardly follow their lead. The company has a robust competitive advantage in its nuclear fuel production capabilities, which should serve it well as the nuclear energy renaissance continues to take shape. For those seeking a leading nuclear energy stock, Centrus remains a worthwhile consideration.

#3068. The United Explorer Card Is Great if You Fly Basic Economy

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Basic economy tickets come with plenty of restrictions, but holding the right credit card is my top trick for avoiding some of them.

As airfares remain high, that basic economy fare might look more tempting than ever. Saving $100 on a round-trip domestic flight could mean more money for hotels, dining or other experiences at your destination.

I’ve flown my fair share of basic economy flights, which come with restrictions like being unable to select a seat, earning no award miles and boarding last. You also can’t make changes or cancel your itinerary without paying a hefty fee, and — specific to United Airlines — you can’t bring a full-size carry-on on board.

But as a United frequent flyer, I have a secret to share with you: Holding the United℠ Explorer Card (annual fee: $0 intro for the first year, then $150) lets you avoid several of those basic economy restrictions.

Right now, new United℠ Explorer Card applicants can receive this welcome offer: Earn 70,000 bonus miles after you spend $3,000 on purchases in the first 3 months your account is open. Plus, earn 10,000 bonus miles when you add an authorized user in the first 3 months your account is open.

Most major airlines no longer let you earn points or miles on basic economy fares. United is an exception, though — assuming you hold the right credit card.

Eligible cardholders, including those who have the United℠ Explorer Card, earn at least 3 miles per $1 spent on United basic economy tickets, excluding taxes and select fees. That’s half of what a standard economy ticket earns for cardholders (6 miles per $1), but it’s better than zero. If you have elite status, you’ll earn more.

Those miles are in addition to the 3 miles per $1 you earn on all United purchases when using your United℠ Explorer Card.

With the card, your miles can become more valuable. That’s because cardholders get at least 10% off award tickets that are operated by United or United Express. If you have United status, that discount gets bumped up to at least 15% off.

With the credit card, you have access to priority boarding in Group 2. That means you’ll be able to board earlier compared to the rest of the basic economy passengers in Group 6, making it more likely you’ll find space for your carry-on bag.

Now, you might be thinking: Wait a minute… Basic economy doesn’t allow you to bring a carry-on bag. Let me explain how that works.

United enforces its full-size carry-on restriction based on your boarding group. Group 6 passengers can't bring a full-size carry-on.

Because the credit card gets you Group 2 boarding, you essentially skip that restriction.

Premier members — who board in Group 2 or earlier — are also exempt from the full-size carry-on restriction when flying basic economy.

If you don’t want to deal with the hassle of a carry-on bag, you can opt to check it instead.

As long as you pay for your ticket using your United℠ Explorer Card, you’ll be able to check your first bag — and your companion’s first bag — for free when traveling on United. (If you’re booking an award ticket, make sure to use your card to pay for the taxes and fees.)

Keep in mind that if the first flight on your itinerary is operated by another airline, that airline’s baggage rules would apply and you would have to pay for your bag.

Of course, holding the right United credit card only gets you so far.

With basic economy, you’ll still need to pay a fee to change or cancel your ticket, and you won’t be able to select a seat for free.

Ultimately, whether basic economy is a good deal depends on a few factors.

If your plans might change, pay up for standard economy. Otherwise, the penalty to change or cancel will end up costing you more than you saved.

Another thing to consider is whether you’re willing to sit in a middle seat. You might be fine with it for a few hours, but what about a long-haul flight to Asia? Everyone’s threshold is different, so you’ll need to decide whether the middle-seat gamble is worth it.

If your plans are firm and you don’t mind a potential middle seat, booking a basic economy ticket can be a solid way to save money. Just book with a United card like, the United℠ Explorer Card, to avoid the biggest downsides.

#3065. Pinterest vs. Snap: Which Media Stock Is a Better Buy Going into 2027?

Pinterest's forward P/E sits far below Snap's, but their very different profit profiles and risk exposures could tip the 2027 decision.

The digital advertising world is shifting from simple social feeds to platforms that drive direct consumer action. Investors deciding between Pinterest (PINS -4.19%) and Snap (SNAP -2.55%) must weigh which strategy wins.

Pinterest serves as a digital corkboard for inspiration and shopping, while Snap focuses on private communication and augmented reality through its Snapchat app. Both companies are competing for a larger share of global ad budgets among the social media stocks landscape, making them an ideal pair for comparison.

Pinterest operates a visual search platform where users find ideas for everything from home decor to fashion. Unlike traditional social media, users often arrive with specific intent, which allows the company to offer full-funnel advertising campaigns to brands and retailers. In its latest annual report, Pinterest noted that it generates revenue primarily through advertising from a wide range of brands, retailers, and agencies across sectors like technology, travel, and financial services.

In its 2025 fiscal year (FY), ended Dec. 31, revenue reached $4.2 billion, representing a growth rate of 15.8% compared to the prior year. This top-line expansion helped the company achieve net income of $416.9 million for the period. The net margin, which shows how much profit a company keeps from every dollar of sales, was 9.9%. This performance shows a clear trend toward profitability compared to previous fiscal periods as the company scales its user base.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio stood at 7.6x, suggesting the company has ample liquid assets to cover its short-term obligations. The debt-to-equity ratio was 0.1x, which compares total debt against the portion of the company owned by shareholders. Free cash flow, the cash remaining after a business pays for its operating costs and capital expenditures, reached $1.3 billion. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 68.6% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Based on its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio was 3.6x, indicating a solid buffer for meeting short-term financial commitments. The debt-to-equity ratio was 2.1x, which indicates that the company's total debt is more than double the value of shareholder equity. Free cash flow for the period was $437.2 million, measuring the cash a company generates after accounting for capital investments. Note that stock-based compensation represented 155% of operating cash flow, meaning reported cash generation is heavily inflated by this non-cash add-back.

Snap also relies substantially on advertising, which accounted for approximately 87% of its total revenue in 2025. The company competes with Meta Platforms, Pinterest, and others for both user time and advertiser interest. Snap is currently managing thousands of pending lawsuits related to teen mental health and addictive design claims. Furthermore, its business depends on the mobile operating systems of providers such as Apple.

Pinterest is more expensive based on its Forward P/E against future earnings estimates, and Snap also has a lower P/S ratio using sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Between Pinterest and Snap, although the latter is cheaper from a valuation perspective, I would buy the former. I used to own shares of Snap and sold them because the company has struggled for years under CEO and co-founder Evan Spiegel. It finally reached profitability in the third quarter of this year, yet in its key North American market, average daily active users declined year over year to 92 million. This suggests Snap is having difficulty retaining users in its most important market.

Pinterest's monthly active users in North America rose 4% year over year to 107 million, indicating rising popularity. Its Q3 global monthly active users of 661 million was an all-time high. The company has also been consistently profitable over the last couple of years, demonstrating superior financial health compared to Snap.

Moreover, Pinterest's value proposition for consumers as a visual discovery engine is different from social media competitors such as Snap and Meta. The company's Q3 revenue of $1.2 billion represented 12% year-over-year growth, but would have been higher if not for European regulators ending tax exemptions on cheap cross-border imports, which forced international sellers to pull back ad spending, causing Pinterest's Q3 revenue in the region to be flat year over year. This headwind should subside over time as sellers adjust to the new tax environment in Europe.

#3062. Intuitive Surgical Placed 546 da Vinci Systems Last Quarter. Why Wall Street's Sell-Off Misses the Point.

Intuitive Surgical sells the da Vinci surgical robot, but that's actually not how it generates most of its revenue.

Intuitive Surgical (ISRG -3.35%) sold off after it reported third-quarter 2026 earnings. And despite the stock starting to bounce back from that drop, it remains down roughly 20% from its 52-week high as of this writing. To be fair, the stock is richly valued, but investors may be missing the long-term story, which is driven not by the sale of the company's da Vinci surgical robot, but by the parts and services sold to support those robots. Here's what you need to know.

Intuitive Surgical's price-to-sales ratio is roughly 14.5x, while its price-to-earnings ratio is over 56x. Those are both very large numbers, which suggest the stock is expensive. So, on an absolute basis, it makes sense that investors are a bit leery of any news perceived as negative. However, the five-year average P/S ratio is 17.2x, with the five-year average P/E at 66x. Thus, relative to the company's own history, it looks cheap and, for more aggressive growth investors, it is probably worth a deep dive.

Recently, investors have been watching Intuitive Surgical like a hawk as competitors enter the market, including medical device giants such as Medtronic (MDT -2.90%) and Johnson & Johnson (JNJ +1.43%). It seems highly likely that Intuitive Surgical's sales of new da Vinci surgical robot systems could slow down. Any hint of a slowdown is likely to be greeted with a stock decline. That's the big-picture story around the stock drop highlighted above.

Long-term growth investors need to think more deeply about Intuitive Surgical's business. For starters, it sold 546 da Vinci surgical systems in the third quarter of 2026, up from 427 a year ago. So growth hasn't stopped. But the real story shows up in the total number of da Vinci systems in use globally: 12,094, up 12% year over year. The number of da Vinci systems is the top of the funnel.

That's because the sale of new da Vinci systems accounts for only about 25% of the company's revenue. The rest comes from services, Instruments, and accessories. In other words, Intuitive Surgical makes more money from supporting its installed base than it does from selling new robots. Which is why you'll want to monitor the number of surgeries performed, as well. In the third quarter of 2026, the number of procedures performed increased by 15%, even though the number of installed da Vinci systems increased by only 12%. The more Intuitive Surgical's robots are used, the more opportunity it has to sell parts and services.

It is easy to see why investors would view Intuitive Surgical as a manufacturing company driven by the sale of its da Vinci robots. In fact, the sale of new robots is important, but it just may not be as important as you think. The real business driver, the flywheel, is the company's sales of parts and services in support of its already large installed base of robots.

Adding more robots to the installed base is vital, but you shouldn't be so focused on what is at the top of the funnel that you miss the big-picture story. While conservative investors and those with a value focus should probably avoid Intuitive Surgical, growth-oriented investors should probably take a deep dive while the stock is 20% below its 52-week high (and relatively cheap by its own valuation history). Indeed, 75% of the top line suggests the business remains on very solid ground. That's doubly true when you consider that surgical robotics is still in the early stages of adoption worldwide.

#3060. We Write About Saving Money; Here’s How We Trick Our Brains Into Doing It

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Even personal finance writers and editors need psychological hacks to save money. Here are the tips we use, and why they work.

While staring at the Container Store cart on my phone at 11 p.m., I did something for the first time that I’d read about a hundred times. Instead of spending $200 on more organizational boxes that I categorically did not need, I transferred that amount to my savings account.

Normally, my willpower is no match for the very real psychological phenomenon called present bias: Our brains light up more when we’re rewarding our current selves than our future selves.

Research has also shown that you imagine your future self almost like a stranger — it’s tougher to care about that stranger than about who you are right now. But that night, I saw near-future-me using that $200 on next summer’s vacation. I wanted the vacation more than the boxes. So I abandoned my cart.

I had managed to use a psychological savings trick: Find a goal that helps you envision your future self. With this epiphany in mind, I asked some of my fellow Nerds to share the savings habits they’ve put to work in their own lives. Perhaps reading these can give late-night you some saving inspiration.

Kimberly Palmer, senior writer and spokesperson: I let nonessential items sit in my online shopping cart for at least 24 hours before buying them. It definitely makes me realize I don't need a lot of them! I also informed my children of this rule, and it has helped cut down on their requests — or at least they know they have to be patient.

Karen Gaudette Brewer, head of content: Fellow let-it-sit-in-the-cart person here. I wait for notifications that something's at least 30% off before I make any moves. Ideally, 40% to 50% off.

Julie Myhre-Nunes, managing editor: When I browse online, I add an item to my shopping cart and wait 24 to 48 hours. After that, I either decide it’s a waste of money/an impulse purchase and delete it from my cart (or forget about the cart altogether), or I decide I want it and make the purchase — it’s the former 99% of the time.

Why it works: “Waiting 1 to 2 days allows me to take the emotion out of purchasing. I no longer succumb to marketing or sales, and instead make spending decisions based on what my family and I actually need or can use.” — Julie again, eloquently making the point for me.

Caitlin Constantine, managing editor: My one real shopping weakness is books, and I’ve found I can get a very close approximation of that feeling without spending an outrageous amount of money by going to the library, wandering around the stacks to see what books might look interesting, and checking out a bunch of them. Even if I don’t end up reading them, I’m still helping the library with circulation numbers, so I see it as a win-win.

Spencer Tierney, senior writer: Plus one to Caitlin Constantine’s “my one real shopping weakness is books.” If I want an older or smaller book, I'll try to find it at one of four libraries I belong to on Libby, the library e-book reader app. (The Libby waitlist for new books can take months, so I have to account for that.)

Why it works: There’s no secret here: It’s nice to get free stuff. You might be surprised what the library has to offer, like movies, magazine subscriptions, games, classes and theater tickets.

Pamela de la Fuente, managing editor: We always tell people to automate savings, and I do in my high-yield savings account, but Dasha Kennedy, the woman behind “The Broke Black Girl”, told Elizabeth Ayoola on the Smart Money podcast that you should invest, even a little bit every week or so, for future you. So I just set up a small recurring deposit to my robo-advisor.

Why it works: You only have to make this decision once for your future self, not over and over again. And if you do it early enough, you could avoid being part of the 41% of Americans who wish they had started investing earlier, according to a 2026 NerdWallet survey.

Athena Cocoves, managing editor: To cut down on food delivery, I started cooking when I feel like it instead of when I’m supposed to. I exercise free will and make soup at 10 p.m. It sounds more obvious than it feels, and it's made a big difference in my budget. And it'd make an even bigger difference if I wasn't also rewarding myself with fancy spices and vinegars, but a girl's gotta splurge somehow.

Ryan Brady, lead writer: My wife takes advantage of a T-Mobile freebie that gives us a discount for DoorDash deliveries. She uses it to buy groceries from Aldi instead of takeout. Aldi is far from us. Publix is right across the street, but more expensive. So this saves us money on the actual items and also helps us not impulse buy (as we often do when we're in-store shopping).

Why it works: You don’t need to call on willpower if you can avoid the situation that tempts you to spend money.

» Read more: Strategies to save on groceries that actually work

Kenley Young, managing editor: I use credit cards for most purchases to get those sweet, sweet rewards, but there's a lot in this article that I've also taken to heart.

I've started using library resources more, and it's saving me money on things like monthly subscriptions.

I've negotiated down several monthly bills, too: refinanced my car loan, got cheaper homeowners insurance, opted for a little less auto coverage in exchange for a lower premium, and haggled online with everyone from Xfinity and SiriusXM to my local newspaper until they all gave me a discount.

Plus, I've opted for some home improvement projects with the expectation that my utility bills would be lower. A plumber finally fixed our toilet, which has led to lower water bills, and I even installed solar panels.

Lastly, my analog skill (playing live music) has become a steady side hustle. I play eight to 10 gigs a month, where (on top of my baseline rate) I also get tips, free food and beer.

Why it works: Because you’re Kenley and you put in a lot of effort.

But you don’t need to be Kenley to find a savings habit that works for your personality, your financial situation and even your musical talents. You just need to experiment. Pick the strategy that speaks to you most, and test it out. Which one are you going to try first?

#3058. This Is the Best-Performing Magnificent Seven Stock of 2026. Is It Still a Buy?

Apple has crushed other members of the Magnificent Seven in 2026.

The "Magnificent Seven" is a grouping of stocks used to describe some of the largest companies in the world that have helped lead the U.S. stock market higher over the past decade. It is made up of:

For U.S.-listed stocks, all seven of these are in the top 10, so being on this list is a big deal. However, 2026 hasn't been the greatest year for the Magnificent Seven. The S&P 500 is up about 12.3% this year, and only three stocks are outperforming it: Apple, Amazon, and Microsoft. But which one of these is doing the best? It's the non-AI one.

The top performer so far in 2026 has been Apple, up about 29%. That's practically double the second-best performer, Amazon, which has risen around 15%. So, what has separated Apple from the competition? I think there are two reasons.

First, Apple really isn't an AI stock. While its products have some AI features, it's not spending hundreds of billions of dollars on data centers like every other company on this list (besides Nvidia, which is selling the equipment for these data centers). As a whole, the market has been a bit bearish on the AI buildout lately, and Apple has been a haven for investors who aren't as keen on the long-term prospects of AI.

In fact, Apple is the only stock among the top 10 largest companies on U.S. exchanges that doesn't have significant AI investments or business cases riding on the AI buildout. This has made it a safe haven for non-AI investors and has boosted its stock.

Another reason Apple has done well this year is that its iPhone 17 lineup was the most popular of any of its previous models. This has driven sales growth and has made Apple a respectable business again.

However, Apple's day of reckoning could be coming. Outside of Tesla (which has a sky-high valuation of 424 times trailing earnings), Apple is by far the most expensive stock in the Magnificent Seven.

As a result, I think the stock could be getting a bit too pricey, and investors need to watch out. Apple's strong performance this year hasn't come entirely from business results, and if the market flips its stance on AI and becomes bullish on it again, Apple's stock could sell off in favor of some AI-focused investments. It's about as good as it gets for Apple sentiment right now. As a result, I don't think Apple stock is a buy right now.

#3055. A Once-in-a-Decade Opportunity: 1 Super Growth Stock Down 58% to Buy and Hold for a Decade

This stock just got crushed, and it's a great opportunity to buy on the dip.

Artificial intelligence (AI) stocks were rising again as investors take in the results of the election, sending the S&P 500 higher.

But that good news didn't extend across the board. Coffee shop chain Dutch Bros (BROS -2.31%) released its third-quarter earnings on Wednesday, and its stock plummeted. It's now a crushing 58% off its high.

However, things could change rapidly for this growth stock, and if you buy it today and hold it for a decade, I have a high conviction that you'll be well-rewarded. Here's why.

Dutch Bros has a casual, down-to-earth vibe with fun, exclusive beverages that differentiate it from other chains. In fact, many of its drinks don't have coffee at all. About 90% of what it sells is cold, and it offers energy drinks and many flavor combinations.

The other parts of the model include a mostly drive-thru store portfolio, a robust digital membership program, and a new food menu.

It's attracting loyal fans wherever it has already opened, which is in 28 states. It opened 48 new shops in the quarter to reach 1,273, and it has 1328 locations as of today.

In Q3, revenue increased 30% year over year, and same-shop sales increased 4.4%. The company has a franchise model, but it has switched to opening only company-owned stores. It has rigorous standardization across its fleet, and company-owned stores increased 5.5%. Net income increased from $27.3 million to $37.2 million.

There were several reasons why the market didn't like the report. First, it's a slowdown from previous quarters. And worse, the company lowered its full-year outlook across metrics. Here are the prior and new numbers for the outlook:

Data source: Dutch Bros quarterly reports.

The company also provided an update that it will meet its goal of opening 185 new stores this year despite a previous bid for a 65-store chain falling through, and it plans to accelerate openings to 250 stores next year.

That wasn't a good enough update to prevent the stock from plunging, but Dutch Bros has serious expansion plans. It expects to have 2,029 stores by 2029, and it reaffirmed that target. It plans to have 7,000 stores over several years, and in a decade from now, that goal is likely to be even bigger.

New stores are leading to scale as the company enters new markets, grows its brand, and gains new customers. Third-party research shows it outperforming industry standards across various guest experience metrics, and it gets the highest customer service ratings.

Dutch Bros has a deliberate real estate strategy, part of which involves opening several stores in one area to build density. That bolsters its presence and brand, and having several stores within customers' reach helps it become part of their everyday routine. That can, however, lead to lower same-store sales growth, since new stores take share from old stores.

Dutch Bros stock trades at a premium, which is why it's been falling. When it's priced for perfection, it can't handle any missteps even if its results were strong. At the current price, Dutch Bros stock trades at 28 times next year's earnings, which I still wouldn't call a bargain.

However, it's an attractive price for a stock with as much potential as Dutch Bros. There could be lots of volatility over the next decade, which is the nature of growth stocks. This looks like a great entry point for risk-tolerant investors who can ignore the noise and hold for many years.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates dropped today, but inflation data could shake things up next week.

With the midterm elections behind us, mortgage rates are continuing their gradual slide this month. One factor is a sell-off in tech stocks, which can send investors toward the relative safety of bonds and help push mortgage rates lower.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.96% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is five basis points lower than yesterday and 11 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Meanwhile, a stronger-than-expected jobs report could slow that slide by reinforcing expectations for another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

And a reminder: While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

The recent slide in mortgage rates might reverse course if next week’s inflation data comes in hot.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday, Nov. 10. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at the other data set, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn healthy: The October jobs report, released today from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Right now, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as expected and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates will be less affected.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.46% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.
