#3050. 2 ETFs I'd Buy in November and Hold for the Next 20 Years

These two funds offer a simple way to invest in businesses around the world.

Want to invest but don't want to spend all your spare time researching the stock market? No need to fear. You could be a perfect candidate for an index exchange-traded fund (ETF). These funds hold a wide variety of stocks, giving investors exposure to a ton of great companies, meaning they can spend less time managing their portfolios.

By owning just two ETFs -- one international and one domestic -- a person can cover the whole globe. Let's learn about how the Vanguard Morningstar Total Stock Market ETF (VTI +0.39%) and the Vanguard Total International Stock ETF (VXUS +0.15%) can serve as an excellent starting point for a long-term investment strategy.

We'll start here: What does VTI do? In short, it owns a broad collection of American stocks, around 3,500 in total. All segments of the U.S. economy are represented, as are stocks of all sizes. Its top holdings include major tech stocks like Nvidia, Apple, and Microsoft. Indeed, its top-10 holdings account for about 35% of the fund's total assets. Beyond these giants, the fund also holds many smaller stocks across sectors like financials, healthcare, industrials, and consumer goods.

The benefit for an investor should be clear: By buying shares of this one ETF, a person gains exposure to thousands of businesses, spreading risk across many stocks and saving tremendous time that would have been spent buying the stocks separately.

That said, the fund's top-heavy nature creates one problem: Much of its performance comes from the big tech stocks that make up a large share of its assets. Those large companies have the greatest influence on the fund's returns. So, in essence, as the tech sector goes, so goes VTI.

In recent years, that has meant excellent returns. VTI has generated a total return of 65% over the last five years, equating to a compound annual growth rate of 10.6%. But if big tech falters, the fund could face turbulence.

To build a truly global stock portfolio, you'll need to look beyond U.S. borders. That's where VXUS comes in. It is an international ETF that holds an eye-popping total of nearly 9,000 stocks headquartered outside the U.S. Geographically, the fund spans continents. The Asia-Pacific region leads with roughly 49% of fund assets, followed by Europe (38%), then North and South America, excluding the U.S. (10%).

Investors should consider adding exposure outside the U.S. because no one knows how the global economy will perform over the next 10 or 20 years. While the U.S. has performed better in recent decades, that trend could reverse. It's best for investors to spread risk across many countries, capturing exposure to many markets, any of which could catch fire.

Granted, owning international stocks still comes with risks. Currency fluctuations, geopolitical risks, and trade and regulatory impacts can all affect returns.

One of the main appeals of index ETFs is their affordability. Almost all ETFs charge fees, expressed as an annual expense ratio. The fees for both VTI and VXUS are some of the lowest in the ETF universe. VTI has an expense ratio of 0.03%, while VXUS' is 0.05%. For context, a person who invests $10,000 in each fund should expect to pay $3 in annual fees for their VTI shares and $5 for their VXUS shares.

One hypothetical portfolio a person could construct would allocate $1,000 across the two funds. Say $600 in VTI and $400 in VXUS. This would give them exposure to both domestic and international stocks, with a slight tilt toward U.S.-based stocks. Furthermore, if a person committed to a regular contribution, say, $100 per month, they could significantly increase their total over decades. Those contributions alone would total $13,000 after 10 years and $25,000 after 20 years.

Broad ownership, low costs, and fewer recurring investment decisions: These are major selling points for a person considering a VTI/VXUS investment strategy. Granted, there are far more elaborate ways to invest, and in many cases, those ways could generate better returns. However, for a person who wants to invest -- and everyone who can absolutely should do it -- a hybrid VTI/VXUS investment strategy is a great starting point. That's why VTI and VXUS are the 2 ETFs I'd buy this November.

Jake Lerch has positions in Nvidia. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple, Microsoft, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3047. Pentair: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

A regional sales restructuring and commercial expansion set the stage for margin recovery as inventory headwinds fade.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Pentair (PNR -2.37%) announced quarterly sales of $976 million and adjusted earnings per share of $1.18. The pool segment completed a channel inventory destock, clearing excess inventory from distribution channels to match factory shipments with end-customer demand.

Three key management insights outline how the business is positioned for long-term growth.

Management finished aligning distributor supply levels with true consumer demand, setting up factory shipments to match retail sales in 2027. This inventory reset removes a major drag on reporting performance without sacrificing underlying customer purchases.

The company completed its purchase of Taco Group Holdings to enter fast-growing heating, ventilation, and air conditioning applications alongside data center water systems. This transaction expands spec-driven replacement sales across schools, hospitals, and commercial buildings.

Pentair reorganized seven sales territories into two regional divisions to adapt equipment marketing, dealer training, and technical service to local market conditions. Direct field support to regional service technicians targets recurring aftermarket replacement sales on installed equipment pads.

Fourth-quarter top-line momentum is picking up as acquisition gains offset softness in residential water treatment, and leadership is clear about the trajectory. The company relies on disciplined product pricing, an established distribution reach, and expanding return on sales, which measures operational profit efficiency, across its core flow and water treatment units.

With distribution inventory stabilized, management expects factory shipments to track end-user demand across expanding commercial cooling and water infrastructure markets. The business model continues to generate substantial operating cash flow to support balance sheet debt reduction while funding core product innovation.

#3043. Novo Nordisk's Wegovy Pill Has Topped 5 Million Prescriptions. Is the Stock a Buy Now?

Novo Nordisk is looking to build a volume-driven business, but the successful launch of the Wegovy pill is just the first step.

Novo Nordisk (NVO +0.03%) now goes by the name Novo, a move intended to highlight the company's changed business focus. Nothing really changed about the company, per se, but as you assess the success of the drugmaker's Wegovy GLP-1 weight-loss pill, you need to understand it isn't running the same game plan as before. Here's why Novo Nordisk could be a buy today for income-focused turnaround investors, but why more conservative investors may still want to wait before jumping aboard.

Novo Nordisk was the first to market with a GLP-1 weight-loss shot. The importance of this category of drugs can't be overstated. Obesity and its health implications are a global problem. Telling people to eat less and exercise more clearly hasn't been enough to solve the issue. But now, there is a medical solution for those who struggle with their weight.

And that doesn't even take into consideration the purely cosmetic value of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs. In fact, early on, Novo Nordisk couldn't keep up with demand because so many patients wanted them. That was a massive problem because it allowed generic competition to enter the market sooner than it otherwise would have. And when Eli Lilly (LLY -0.79%) brought out GLP-1 drugs that offered greater weight loss, Novo Nordisk quickly became the industry also-ran.

This is why it is so important that Novo Nordisk's GLP-1 pill is seeing such strong acceptance. The pill has now reached more than five million patients. And its launch has been even more successful than the company's Wegovy shot. But don't rush out and buy the stock just yet.

Novo Nordisk's shift to just Novo is part of a change that focuses the company on selling more drugs at lower prices. So, despite the success of the Wegovy pill, Novo Nordisk's earnings have been relatively weak, as selling at lower prices has reduced revenues and earnings. That's part of the plan, so the company is doing exactly what it said it would. But it still isn't clear that a volume-driven model will be sustainable over the long term for this pharma giant.

The healthcare sector is highly competitive, and Eli Lilly has already launched its own GLP-1 pill. Meanwhile, other competitors are working on rival weight-loss drugs of their own. To be fair, the global weight-loss opportunity is huge, so there is likely to be room for more than one GLP-1 drug. But for more conservative investors, waiting for Novo Nordisk to show a little more progress on the income statement may be the more desirable approach.

That said, there is an income opportunity here for more aggressive investors willing to step into a turnaround story. Novo Nordisk's dividend yield is highly attractive at 4.4%, and its trailing 12-month payout ratio is a comfortable 33%. While 2026 is a transition year, the early success of the Wegovy pill hints that the transition to a volume-driven model is going reasonably well. If you are willing to give this company the benefit of the doubt, you could end up with a big yield and capital appreciation.

There's no way to know what happens in the future, so you have to make an educated guess about whether or not Novo Nordisk will be able to shift to a volume-driven model with its GLP-1 pill. Given how quickly things turned south for Novo Nordisk's GLP-1 shot, there's always a chance that the good news comes to an abrupt end.

However, so far, the news is good, and more aggressive dividend investors will probably find the stock attractive. Notably, the stock's price-to-sales and price-to-earnings ratios are half their five-year averages, so it looks historically cheap. Add a value price tag to a high, well-covered yield, and the strong Wegovy pill launch starts to make this drug maker look a lot more appealing.

#3040. Should You Buy Meta Platforms Before Its Stock Split?

Meta Platforms is creating a new share class as part of its upcoming stock split.

Meta Platforms (META +0.84%) has been an up-and-down stock in 2026, and its latest announcement may just add more vigor to its oscillations: The company will enact a 2-for-1 stock split in January. However, this won't be your standard stock split, and it comes with nuances investors need to be aware of.

So, should you buy Meta's stock before the event arrives? After all, stocks often run up significantly when the market is waiting for them to split.

Meta's stock split isn't exactly standard. Instead of splitting its shares 2-for-1 outright, it's creating a new class of shares. For each pre-split Class A share an investor owns, they will be given one Class A share and one new Class C share. The A shares will have voting rights. The C shares won't. This may seem like an odd way to split the stock, but it's easy to see what Meta is trying to do. Meta has told investors that in the future, it will use C shares for employee compensation, acquisitions, and raising cash.

So, with each new C share issued, the shareholder base grows, but the new stock structure locks in majority voting power for CEO and founder Mark Zuckerberg. He already holds more than half the voting power thanks to the super-voting Class B shares that he owns 99.7% of -- those have 10 votes apiece. Now, he can further dilute his 13% financial stake in Meta by issuing more shares without diluting his control of it. He thus can continue to steer the company in any direction he wants without being concerned that the other shareholders might replace him, barring any malpractice.

Bearing in mind that Meta's stock split also locks the company into Zuckerberg's vision for the foreseeable future, the question of whether you should buy it now hinges on whether you trust his long-term vision. If you do, then this move makes a ton of sense. If you don't, now may be the time for you to get Meta's stock out of your portfolio.

However, Zuckerberg's current direction for the company has started to pay off, as its recently launched agentic AI app, Meta Muse, is seeing huge early adoption and could prove to be the very platform the company needs to start making some direct profits from all of its AI spending. Overall, Meta's business is still doing great, with revenue rising 26% year over year. So, Meta is still doing great as a company.

But because it's not yet clear what its path to monetizing AI will look like, the market is still viewing the stock with skepticism. This is reflected in its 1-year forward price-to-earnings ratio of 18.

META PE Ratio (Forward 1y) data by YCharts.

That's not a particularly high valuation for the company, especially considering the reaction the market has had since Meta reported its Q3 results. If you want to buy, now could be a prime opportunity because it's on sale. However, if you're not sure about Zuckerberg, it's time to move on, because he's about to lock in his majority voting power permanently.

Keithen Drury has positions in Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3036. Why Nov. 9 Could Be a Huge Day for Rocket Lab Investors

Can Rocket Lab's next big business update help spur a rebound for its beaten-down stock?

Rocket Lab (RKLB +15.39%) is poised to report its third-quarter results after the market closes on Nov. 9, which is shaping up to be a huge moment for the stock. The company's share price has been under pressure due to a variety of factors, and investors will be looking to the space-tech specialist's Q3 report to gauge the business's near-term valuation outlook.

As of this writing, Rocket Lab stock is down 24% in 2026 and 64% from the all-time high it reached earlier in the year. To put the significance of the company's upcoming quarterly report in perspective, it's worth examining why the stock has experienced such a precipitous pullback.

Some of the big pullbacks are connected to bearish trends in the broader market, driven by deflating valuations for artificial intelligence stocks, but investors also appear concerned that space companies could face less favorable operating conditions amid a shifting balance of political power.

#3032. Warren Buffett Has 1 Simple Rule for Surviving a Stock Market Crash, and History Says It's Never Once Failed

If a bear market is coming, Warren Buffett's advice is more important than ever.

It's been a roller-coaster of a week for the stock market. The Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.09%) officially entered correction territory after falling by more than 10% from its October peak, while the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%) and Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.46%) are down by around 6% and 5%, respectively.

Investors are also grappling with the downturn. The Fear and Greed index measures investor sentiment based on multiple stock market metrics, and it quickly plunged into the "extreme fear" category amid this week's turbulence.

But it's not all bad news right now. Warren Buffett has some timeless advice for investing during crashes and bear markets, and history says this strategy has worked out every single time.

When the market is shaky, it can be tempting to avoid investing or even sell your stocks in fear that prices will fall further. According to Warren Buffett, though, that's one of the worst moves to make during a slump.

"A simple rule dictates my buying," Buffett explained in a 2008 opinion piece for The New York Times. "Be fearful when others are greedy, and be greedy when others are fearful." He went on to emphasize that "bad news is an investor's best friend. It lets you buy a slice of America's future at a marked-down price." In other words, when the future is bleak, buy more.

In the years following the publication of that piece, the S&P 500 soared by nearly 1,000%, and the investors who reaped the biggest rewards were those who stayed invested even when it was tough.

"Over the long term, the stock market news will be good," Buffett continued, noting the Dow's extraordinary rise from 66 to 11,497 during the 20th century.

"You might think it would have been impossible for an investor to lose money during a century marked by such an extraordinary gain. But some investors did. The hapless ones bought stocks only when they felt comfort in doing so and then proceeded to sell when the headlines made them queasy."

No matter how severe the short-term volatility is, the market has always managed to recover.

During the dot-com bubble in 2000, for example, the S&P 500 fell by nearly 50% as the tech sector collapsed. It took until 2007 for the index to reach another all-time high, and just a few months later, the Great Recession began.

However, between 2000 and 2020, the S&P 500 still earned total returns of more than 224%. In other words, even if you'd invested at seemingly the worst moment -- immediately before the onset of two of the worst recessions in U.S. history -- you'd still have more than tripled your money over 20 years.

This trend holds up throughout history, too. Between 1919 and 2025, all of the S&P 500's rolling 20-year periods have ended in positive total returns, according to analysis from Crestmont Research. By holding an S&P 500 ETF or index fund for 20 years, it's historically been harder to lose money in the stock market than to make money.

Regardless of what's ahead for the market, continuing to invest is key. However, it's more important than ever to choose your stocks wisely. Weak companies can thrive during periods of economic prosperity, but recessions and bear markets will hit those stocks the hardest. The weakest of them may crash and never recover at all.

Companies with strong fundamentals -- such as a durable competitive advantage, healthy finances, and a competent leadership team -- are the most likely to rebound after a crash. The more of these stocks you own, the better prepared you'll be to not only survive a downturn, but thrive over time.

Katie Brockman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3028. Here's What the Average Man's Social Security Benefit Looks Like by Age

Men's average retirement benefit runs $409.85 higher monthly than women's, a gap rooted in lifetime earnings with survivor benefit implications.

Men's average Social Security benefits run higher than women's at every age. This breakdown lays out the specific figures across the early retirement years based on the most recent data snapshot.

As of December 2025, the average retirement benefit among men is $2,282.28 per month, according to the Social Security Administration (SSA). The average for women is $1,872.43, which leaves a monthly gap of $409.85.

The SSA bases your benefit on an average of your wages across your 35 highest-earning years. These wages are adjusted for inflation and run through a formula designed to replace about 40% of your pre-retirement income. Most retirees need a much higher share of their prior pay to cover their costs.

These averages represent a snapshot of current beneficiaries, including those who claimed early and those who waited for higher payments. As of December 2025, the monthly averages show consistent differences between genders across the board.

The higher average for men reflects historical differences in lifetime earnings and years spent in the paid workforce. While individual work histories vary, these broader patterns shape the current benefit landscape across every age group.

A man who is 65 today is expected to live about 18 more years, while a woman is expected to live about 21 more years, according to the SSA. This means that men's Social Security benefits typically cover a larger proportion of their retirement costs than women's, who have to pay for more years of living expenses with smaller checks.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027. The above figures don't yet reflect that increase, which will add about $91 a month to the $2,529.62 average for 70-year-old men. Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later.

Married people have the option to claim a spousal benefit on their partner's work record while their partner is still alive and a survivor benefit after their partner's death. You only get these checks if they're worth more than your own retirement benefit.

Since women have historically had fewer years in the workforce and lower average earnings, they're more likely to claim spousal and survivor benefits, which means married men also need to think about how their claiming decision could affect their spouse.

Your partner cannot claim a spousal benefit until you are receiving your retirement checks, so your claiming age may affect theirs. Talk your options over with your spouse and make sure you're both on the same page about when each of you plans to sign up. If one of you wants to change things up down the road, keep the other in the loop and adjust your retirement plan accordingly.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

#3023. Why ASML Holdings Fell in October

A big earnings beat wasn't enough to offset broader fears that drove the AI stock sell-off last month and into November.

Shares of ASML Holdings (ASML +5.02%) fell 11.5% in October, according to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence.

ASML reported solid third-quarter results mid-month and gave a strong Q4 guide; alas, these strong earnings weren't enough to offset the extreme sell-off in AI-related stocks, especially semiconductor stocks, ahead of the November midterms.

ASML holds a monopoly on extreme ultraviolet (EUV) technology, which is required to produce all leading-edge logic and memory chips that power the AI revolution. As such, there isn't really a debate about whether ASML has strong near-term growth, but rather about how much growth there will be, and for how long.

In the third quarter, ASML revenue grew 55.2% over the prior year to 11.7 billion Euros, while adjusted earnings per share rose 135.9% to 12.53 Euros per share. Revenue actually slightly missed expectations, but ASML's bottom line beat on expanding margins. Moreover, ASML guided for fourth-quarter numbers well ahead of expectations, with revenue in the range of 14.2 billion to 15.2 billion Euros, while maintaining its higher-than-historical margins.

Not only that, but ASML also said that its customers' demand forecasts had increased so much that it was looking to expand capacity in 2028 by more than the 30% it was already planning.

Yet even all this good news was overwhelmed by anti-AI stock sentiment that took hold in October and continued into November. Fears over the sustainability of growth at leading AI labs, along with consternation over the impact of a Democratic "wave" election, appeared to overshadow ASML's strong results.

After the Democratic "wave" last week, AI-related semiconductor stocks sold off hard, and now appear to be pricing in the end of the current cycle next year or in 2028.

However, this may be an overblown reaction. After all, not even a month ago, ASML had conveyed that its customers were seeing stronger demand for longer. If the build-out continues with only mild modifications, then ASML is a strong buy on this dip.

#3018. The Top 5 Biotech Stocks I'd Buy Right Now Without Any Hesitation

These are among the best the industry has to offer.

Biotech stocks have performed well in 2026, but things could change fairly rapidly. The industry is notoriously volatile, after all, and there are significant regulatory issues that may weigh on biotech companies. However, regardless of what happens over the next year or two, there are several biotechs with attractive prospects that are worth investing in for the long haul. Let's consider five of them.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX +0.85%) has long been the leader in the cystic fibrosis (CF) medicines market, and the company's core therapeutic area remains a meaningful growth driver, with no major patent cliff expected over the next 10 years. However, the company is also making strides elsewhere. Consider Vertex's efforts in kidney diseases. It is inching closer to an approval for povetacicept, a medicine for IgA nephropathy.

Its Phase 3 pipeline also features inaxaplin, an investigational drug for APOL-1-mediated kidney disease. The biotech boasts several recent approvals whose sales should ramp up over the next few years, and it has also diversified its pipeline. Vertex's dominance in its core market and progress elsewhere make the stock a buy.

While Vertex's growth comes primarily from a single therapeutic area, Amgen (AMGN +0.03%) has taken a different approach. In the third quarter, the company had 17 products that delivered at least double-digit year-over-year sales growth. It also had 17 medicines with at least $1 billion annual revenue run rate. That's impressive, and it helps explain why Amgen has been able to overcome recent major patent cliffs without too much trouble, including the one for denosumab, a medicine for several bone-related conditions.

The drugmaker's deep pipeline, which features MariTide -- a highly promising weight-loss medicine -- should enable it to extend its approved lineup. Finally, Amgen is also an excellent dividend stock, having increased its payouts every year since initiating them in 2011.

Gilead Sciences' (GILD -0.41%) portfolio of medicines features some of the best-selling HIV drugs, such as Biktarvy and Descovy for PrEP. The company still generates the bulk of its sales from this area, and one recent approval has helped strengthen the company's position. Gilead Sciences earned approval for Bixlenvo, an HIV pill. However, Gilead Sciences has also made headway in other areas, particularly oncology, in recent years.

Gilead Sciences is betting big on its cancer drug pipeline, which is larger than its HIV pipeline. And as it launches new products in this area, the company will have a stronger, more diversified lineup. Meanwhile, revenue and earnings growth remain strong for the company, and the dividend is rock solid, too. Gilead Sciences has increased its payouts by 74.5% over the past decade.

Exelixis (EXEL +1.25%) isn't the most popular biotech company. It is much smaller than all of the others discussed here. However, Exelixis has managed to become successful in a corner of the vast and highly competitive oncology market. The company's most important medicine, Cabometyx, has earned multiple indications across multiple cancer types.

Cabometyx will likely face generic competition in the U.S. by early 2030, but Exelixis is ready for it. The company could earn approval for zanzalintinib, an investigational medicine for colorectal cancer, next year. It will target additional indications while also developing new cancer medicines. Exelixis' position in oncology makes it an attractive biotech to buy.

Regeneron Pharmaceuticals (REGN +0.16%) has encountered stiff competition for Eylea, a medicine that treats several eye-related diseases, in recent years. This has harmed the company's financial results. However, the drugmaker is slowly putting this issue in the rearview mirror. Revenue and earnings growth have rebounded, largely thanks to a new formulation of Eylea and Dupixent, a medicine for eczema. Regeneron's long-term plans feature both the new version of Eylea and Dupixent.

Dupixent may run into a patent cliff early next decade, but Regeneron will likely seek to protect its empire, notably by developing a high-dose formulation of Dupixent. The company also boasts an attractive pipeline of programs to diversify its lineup, including promising weight-loss candidates. Regeneron's comeback may just be getting started. It's still time to buy the stock.

Prosper Junior Bakiny has positions in Exelixis and Vertex Pharmaceuticals. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amgen, Exelixis, Gilead Sciences, Regeneron Pharmaceuticals, and Vertex Pharmaceuticals. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3008. Ohio, Georgia, and Arizona Rank Right Behind Texas for New Data Centers, and Democrats Won All 3

Texas isn't the only major data center hub that got a little more blue after the midterms.

All eyes were on Texas during the midterms, and artificial intelligence (AI) stocks, especially the neoclouds, took a beating when a Democrat governor won the state. The main fear is that increased AI regulations will make it harder to build data centers in the future, and existing projects may get delayed.

It's a legitimate concern, and Texas isn't the only state that was affected. Ohio, Georgia, and Arizona also saw Democratic victories, and they have been instrumental states for the AI boom. Bears will argue that Democratic victories can turn into a major bottleneck for AI as construction projects are delayed or canceled. Here's what investors should consider.

While governor victories capture headlines, each governor must still answer to their state's House and Senate. Those two political arms introduce more variety that can result in gridlock instead of sweeping AI regulations. Here's how it varies for the four states that have played a vital role in artificial intelligence:

Ohio has the best political profile for tech companies. A gridlock essentially lets existing data center projects continue. Texas and Georgia can still create a gridlock due to Republicans controlling the Senate, but their political leverage isn't as good as Ohio's.

Data centers in Arizona won't face the same political gridlock since Democrats have secured the House and the Senate. Arizona Democrats are in the best position to act swiftly.

Although activism and protests toward data centers have gotten louder in recent months, delaying and canceling these projects comes with downsides that extend beyond lost innovation.

While an anti-big tech stance may cause people to look the other way when hyperscalers face roadblocks, those same data center delays and cancellations have a real impact on jobs.

Politicians on both sides of the aisle love to tout job growth. For instance, former President Biden was enthusiastic about a $3.3 billion data center in Wisconsin since it could create 2,000 permanent jobs once built.

If many data centers are delayed or canceled, it can lead to thousands of people losing their jobs. While regulation may become stricter, kneecapping the industry also translates into lost jobs.

Democrats may not care as much about GDP during this cycle since Trump is still the president, but slowing down AI infrastructure can result in negative GDP growth, since it's the main catalyst powering the economy. A negative GDP growth reading won't be a good look. The Democrats can introduce measures that make their voters happy without uprooting the entire AI trade.

Good PR will be incredibly valuable for neoclouds as the Democrats gain more control of the government in January. Investors can expect to see companies take more steps to financially support local communities beyond new jobs. These initiatives can help them win over residents and provide tangible benefits to the entire community.

Part of that may mean reduced tax breaks, a point of contention that was gaining steam before the midterm results. Some tech companies are also acquiring old infrastructure and converting it into AI data centers. For instance, Terawulf is turning a retired coal plant into an AI data center.

Those types of changes are less disruptive and let AI data center developers focus on job creation. If more neoclouds and colocation developers take this approach, it will be harder for Democrats to issue sweeping changes that stall out the industry. Investors should pay attention to how data center developers are enhancing local communities, because winning over locals will reduce uncertainty with their gigawatt pipelines.

#2999. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

#2993. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2991. Cavs lose to Knicks 109-97 as Brunson and Towns combine for 60 points

The Cleveland Cavaliers lost to the New York Knicks 109-97. Donovan Mitchell finished with a season-high 37 points.

The Cavs started strong for the second night in a row, showing active hands defensively in the first quarter to force three Knicks turnovers. They also held the Knicks to just 3-of-11 three-point shooting in the opening quarter.

Karl-Anthony Towns kept New York attached. He scored 14 points in the first quarter while throwing down a pair of thunderous dunks. This was a much more difficult matchup for Mobley than the previous night, when he held Joel Embiid to 0-of-9 shooting.

A Knicks run led by Jalen Brunson put New York back in front going into halftime. Brunson hit tough shots en route to 16 points in a five-minute stretch. He finished the first half with 20 points.

Cleveland trailed 63-53 at halftime. Then the Knicks blew it open in the third quarter to extend an 18-point lead into the final frame.

New York's quick-hitting offense, which thrives on off-ball movement and screening, had the Cavs perpetually lost throughout the night. James Harden struggled to navigate screens, while the rest of the Cavaliers (even Evan Mobley) weren't able to communicate cleanly enough to avoid giving up opportunities at the rim.

This proved to be a bad combination for the Cavs in last year's playoffs, and it looks like their personnel still isn't able to keep up with New York's offense.

Towns and Brunson ended with a combined 60 points. The Cavs ended this game needing far more from Evan Mobley and James Harden. The two combined for just 19 points. It felt like Mitchell was the only Cavalier who could find his shot for most of the night.

Of course, the Cavs were without some key players tonight. Jarrett Allen missed his third straight game, while Jaylon Tyson has been sidelined since before Halloween. Sam Merrill, who injured his elbow in Friday's game, was also out tonight.

Still, there's a larger trend of the Knicks simply outplaying the Cavs. Sometimes a team has your number, and it feels like the Knicks have held an advantage over Cleveland since 2023.

#2982. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2979. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.

#2977. Mississippi Senate: Colom increases lead over Hyde-Smith to nearly 2,500 votes

Democratic challenger Scott Colom expanded his narrow lead over Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate race on Friday afternoon.

As of 5:30 p.m. ET, Colom led Hyde-Smith by 2,498 votes, out of a total of 953,222 counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press.

That was 685 votes more than Colom's lead over Hyde-Smith when the day began.

Ty Pinkins, an independent candidate, had 20,028 votes, or 2.10% of the total.

The AP estimated that as of 4:45 p.m. ET, there were close to 30,000 votes that remained to be counted.

The Senate elections in Mississippi and Alaska are the only Senate races yet to be called by the AP. Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats from Republicans, meaning they will hold at least 54 seats out of the 100 in the Senate in January.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is leading incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan by 637 votes, a margin of just 0.29 percentage points. Peltola has slightly more than 48% of the votes cast, while Sullivan has slightly less than that.

Pinkins has told media outlets that he realized after a poll in April that he could not win the race, but that he decided to stay in to draw votes from Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins, who was the 2024 Democratic nominee in the Mississippi Senate race against Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, said a number of Republicans had contacted him to say they planned to vote for him because they did not want to vote for Hyde-Smith.

A final result may not be available until next week because more ballots can still arrive and be counted.

Mississippi's election procedures do not have an automatic recount provision.

Hyde-Smith's campaign set the stage for a potential legal battle over ballots on Thursday by demanding in a letter to county commissioners and circuit clerks in all 82 Mississippi counties that they immediately separate all provisional ballots cast in the election from regular and absentee ballots.

"These measures are necessary to ensure that each affidavit ballot's eligibility for counting and ultimate disposition can be verified," the letter said.

Lawyers for Colom's campaign responded with a letter of their own to the counties, calling the Republican's demands "unlawful and extraordinary measures."

"Those demands have no basis in Mississippi law, and if you depart from ordinary election procedures as you have been asked to do, you will violate Mississippi voters' rights and Mississippi election law," they said.

Provisional ballots, which are also known as affidavit ballots, are cast by a voter whose name does not appear on the poll book, who does not present an acceptable photo ID, or in several other situations.

The ballot is then placed in a sealed envelope and later examined by election officials to see if it can be cast, or the voter can return with a photo ID or evidence within five business days to show they are entitled to cast the ballot.

"Absolutely, I didn't think it would be this close," said Brian Anderson, a political science professor at the Mississippi University for Women in Columbus.

Anderson said that before the election, he thought Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, might get into position to lose by just 3 or 4 percentage points, which would have been a very solid showing in a state that hasn't elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982.

"I'm much more surprised" now that Colom has a chance to win, Anderson said.

If Colom defeats Hyde-Smith, 67, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

"The Democrats have struggled here to create a pipeline of candidates," Anderson said. A Colom win "would galvanize the Democratic Party in a way it has not been," he said.

Neither candidate's campaign immediately responded to requests for comment from CNBC.

Hyde-Smith's X account, in a post Thursday afternoon, said, "Cindy Hyde-Smith is going to represent Mississippi in the United States Senate for the next six years."

"We believe thousands of votes remain to be counted from dozens of Republican counties, and we are confident those votes will reinforce her path to victory," the post said.

"Election officials are continuing the normal process of counting outstanding ballots, including absentee ballots. They must have the time to complete that work and ensure every lawful ballot is accurately counted and every Mississippian's voice is heard."

The post added, "We expect Democratic lawyers to pull out every stop, and we are prepared. Our focus is on completing the count and returning Cindy Hyde-Smith to the Senate to keep fighting for Mississippi values."

"Mississippi is the firewall, and this race is our line in the sand," the message said.

In a pair of ironic twists, if Colom wins, his victory could be attributed in some ways to actions taken by two Republicans: President Donald Trump and Hyde-Smith herself.

Supreme Court Justice Amy Coney Barrett, whom Trump appointed, wrote the court's 5-4 majority ruling in June that upheld a Mississippi law allowing mail-in absentee ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if they are received within five business days.

Those ballots could provide Colom a margin of victory. There are also an estimated 10,000 to 15,000 provisional ballots that require verification of eligibility to vote.

A lawyer for the Republican National Committee, whose lawsuit argued federal law barred counting votes received after Election Day, noted in a brief to the Supreme Court, "Democratic voters tend to mail their ballots later on average than Republican voters, which results in late-arriving ballots favoring Democratic candidates."

Trump at the time of the Supreme Court ruling called it "a tremendous loss" for voters' rights.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to her Senate seat in 2018 after the resignation of Thad Cochran and later that year won a special election for the seat. She was elected to a full term in 2020.

In 2023, she effectively blocked Colom's nomination by then-President Joe Biden to a post as a federal judge for the Northern District of Mississippi. Colom's nomination had the support of Mississippi's other Republican senator, Roger Wicker, and two former GOP governors.

Hyde-Smith said she opposed Colom's nomination for reasons that included his purported support for transgender students participating in girls' sports and because his campaign for district attorney had allegedly received money from billionaire George Soros.

Colom, in an April 10, 2023, letter to Hyde-Smith, said he was unaware before his hometown newspaper reported it that Soros had donated to a political action committee that had supported his campaign. He also wrote that he was not sure why Hyde-Smith had suggested he "opposed legislation to protect female athletes."

Colom concluded the letter by promising to be guided by the law, not his personal views on public policy, if he were confirmed as a judge.

"I hope you will reconsider your views about my nomination," he wrote.

Hyde-Smith did not reconsider her views and maintained her block on the nomination.

Colom announced his candidacy for the Democratic nomination for Senate in September 2025 and recently told CNN, "Me not having a federal judgeship is not a motivating factor for me at all" in running.

Anderson said Hyde-Smith ran "a low-visibility campaign," at least compared to Colom's, and that she focused on voters who traditionally would be expected to support her.

Hyde-Smith refused to debate Colom several times.

Colom "knows how to speak to people's interests, and Hyde-Smith was all about values, which is great for a lot of people, but it's all about the interests right now," Anderson said.

Those interests include concerns about affordability, the Iran war and corruption.

Colom is "bringing up all those," Anderson said.

Anderson noted that Colom's campaign had pounced on a remark Hyde-Smith made during a Feb. 22 interview with a local television outlet.

In the interview, Hyde-Smith answered a question about rising beef prices by saying, "You know, there so many proteins to choose from."

Colom's campaign featured a video clip of that remark in an ad featuring people reacting incredulously to Hyde-Smith's suggestion.

"Pick another protein?" one woman said in the ad. "She done lost her d--- mind. I'd rather pick another senator."

#2975. Nets let another one slip away, fall to Hawks in heartbreaking fashion

For nearly 47 minutes, the Brooklyn Nets controlled the game against the Atlanta Hawks Saturday night in Georgia. After building a 17-point lead in the first quarter — it wasn't always pretty — but the Nets didn't trail until the final two minutes.

However, they couldn't put it away when it mattered most and fell to Atlanta, 124-121. There were some positives, most notably Nolan Traore's fast start and the late-game heroics from Mikel Brown Jr. as well as Day'Ron Sharpe's first triple-double. All of that kept them in the game but ultimately, none of it changes the result, and Brooklyn is now 2-7, still struggling to close out matchups down the stretch.

Brooklyn couldn't have asked for a much better start. They came out firing, putting up 37 first-quarter points to take a 37-27 lead. The Nets shot 73.7% from the field (14-of-19), including 6-of-10 from three, led by the fast-rising French point guard…

The ball movement was also on full display early, dishing out 11 assists on 14 made baskets. Eight different players found the scoring column, with the Nets building a lead as large as 17 before Atlanta started to chip away at the end of the frame.

Of course, it wasn't going to be that easy for the rest of the game. By the end of the first, you could already feel Atlanta gaining some ground after an ugly first few minutes. In the second quarter, they trimmed their deficit to just four, at 70-66 entering the break. Turnovers became a big issue for the Nets, allowing the Hawks to stay in the game.

The Nets committed 11 turnovers in the first half, which gave the Hawks 15 points. As Jordi Fernandez noted post-game, it was a game long issue.

"We were not consistent enough to do the right things," said the head coach And it's not about making shots. It's about, you know, trying to do the right things. Obviously, the points off turnovers, like I said before, this is a team that you know will pressure you. And we had 22 turnovers for 28 points. And that's not acceptable. That's where you lose the game."

Brooklyn also got dominated in the paint, allowing a whopping 42 points on the inside compared to their 22. Atlanta put up 39 paint points in the second quarter alone.

Despite the turnovers, the Nets still scored at a high level. Michael Porter Jr. had 18 in the first half, while Nolan Traoré continued his excellent start to the season with 15 points on 6-of-7 shooting. Day'Ron Sharpe also thrived as a facilitator, logging eight assists in the first half. He had set a career high with eight assists on Wednesday, and that number rose to 13 on Saturday, when he also came away with his first triple-double, logging 12 points and 10 rebounds. All positive, but…

The crazy part is that the Nets shot 65.7% from the field and 50% from three in the first half, yet still had just a four-point lead. When you turn the. ball over that many times, that's what happens.

The Nets started to settle things down coming out of the locker room, though. They won the third quarter, 25-22, to take a 95-88 lead heading into the final frame. While the same level of offensive efficiency wasn't there, Brooklyn's defense became more consistent and prevented the Hawks from carrying their momentum into the second half.

The turnover issues remained prevalent for the Nets, as the total reached 16 through three quarters. However, they limited the damage on the defensive end. Fernández clearly made adjustments coming out of halftime, and those adjustments played a key role in Brooklyn retaining their lead.

Despite struggling offensively through the first three quarters and missing his first seven shots, Mikel Brown Jr. came up clutch in the fourth quarter with three huge shots from deep. After entering the final quarter with just five points, he finished with 18.

However, Fernandez decided against playing his two 20-year-olds together. While Brown Jr. was leading the charge, Traore sat on the bench for the entire fourth quarter. Was it defense or does the head coach have a problem playing the two together? Fernandez admitted he went with the hot hand — Brown Jr. — rather than play his two 20-year-olds together.

"I thought that Mikel had those five straight points, and I just let him roll. Because you cannot just keep taking them in and out." he argued. "So I thought that that was the right thing to do. Was it right? I don't know."

After the game, Traore was asked if the head coach had given him any rationale for not playing.

No matter what the young Nets couldn't finish. Traore had his thoughts on why in the same post-game interview:

We played really well in the first half, but we started playing in a disorganized way in the second." he said. "We need to be better on the boards because we could have put the game away. But we weren't good enough."

While the game was back-and-forth throughout, the Nets didn't give up their first lead until there was 1:26 left in the fourth quarter. The game hadn't even been tied; Brooklyn led the entire time until then. After that, though, the Nets failed to score another point and fell 124-121. The Hawks, led by Jalen Johnson, closed the game with a 14-7 run.

Fernandez said that with such a young team, it's a work in progress.

"I'm happy with what I'm seeing and the commitment and playing hard, and now the communication is way better," he said. "It seems more like a connected group. We're rebounding better, especially defensively. So, all those things are important. And it's a testament to the work that the guys put in, the coaches, and getting one percent better every day."

Saturday was a disappointing loss for the Nets. Going from up 17 and not down once to losing in the final two minutes is rough, and yet another example that Brooklyn's young core is still developing and learning how to win games. The turnovers also continued to hurt them, as they committed six more in the final frame.

The encouraging element, of course, is Brown Jr.'s strong play, especially down the stretch. It's the second time he's really risen to the occasion in big moments, with last week's loss to the Boston Celtics as another example before his rookie blunder cost them in overtime.

Final Score: Atlanta Hawks, 124, Brooklyn Nets, 121.

It was a big night for Day'Ron Sharpe. The 6'10" 25-year-old recorded his first career triple-double and the first triple-double ever by a Nets center. Sharpe finished with 12 points, 1o rebounds and 13 assists, the final figure also a record for Nets 5. It was also the Nets first triple-double in nearly a year. Nic Claxton who Sharpe replaced in the starting line up this season had a game of 14 points, 1o rebounds and 10 assists last December.

The Nets will be without both Egor Demin and Mikel Brown Jr. Sunday vs. the Mavs. Since it's a back-to-back, Brown Jr. will sit and Demin is still not ready after suffering a bone bruise vs the Celtics on October 27. Two days later, the Nets announced he'd be reevaluated after one week.

Dëmin will miss a sixth straight game Sunday then be reevaluated by the Nets.

"Yeah, he's progressing well," Jordi Fernández said. "He's on the court. He's not going to play [Saturday] or [Sunday], and then we'll go from there."

The Nets will have a quick turnaround, heading to Dallas on Sunday to face the Mavericks in the second leg of their back-to-back. Dallas enters the matchup at 5-4, coming off a 112-100 victory over the Utah Jazz on Friday.

After letting a golden opportunity slip away against Atlanta, the Nets will look to get back in the win column on Sunday. The question becomes: can Brooklyn put together a complete 48-minute performance to secure the victory? That's what they've struggled to do most consistently this season.

#2973. Tech companies face mounting child safety lawsuits while pushing AI into schools

Tech companies have spent years telling parents they're working to make their products safer for children. But ongoing jury trials, settlements and related litigation against the likes of Meta, Google and Snap suggest that they're not doing enough.

The latest examples landed this week. Snap confidentially settled a case claiming that its technology caused severe psychological distress to two teenage users. Meta, TikTok and YouTube previously settled with the plaintiffs in the same suit. And Google was sued this week by the parents of a child who allegedly suffered a psychological crisis after chatting with the Gemini AI chatbot in Google Classroom.

Concern is intensifying as artificial intelligence companies pursue trillion-dollar valuations, and established internet giants pour money into developing advanced AI models while pushing them into the classroom. Products and features intended to maximize engagement can have detrimental effects on users, thus warranting "broader systemic changes in tech design, particularly when children are using them," said developmental psychologist Kate Blocker.

"I think it's so so important that children's safety is being considered when these products — AI, social media, et cetera — are being designed, and not after the fact as these problems emerge," said Blocker, the director of research and programs for the nonprofit Children and Screens.

Snap's settlement this week, which spared CEO Evan Spiegel from testifying in a Los Angeles court, continues a general trend of tech companies attempting to shield themselves from public scrutiny due to the various high-profile child-safety related trials this year, said Joseph McNally, the director of emerging litigation at McNicholas & McNicholas.

The case was one of several lawsuits related to the social media personal injury trial that Meta and YouTube lost earlier this year. More trials are slated to commence in March.

"I view this settlement and the other settlements in this space as an effort by tech companies to turn a corner, not only because I think that there were real legal challenges, but also the reputation of their products, the reputation of their companies has really been hit," said McNally, a former acting U.S. attorney for the Central District of California.

"The parties are pleased to have been able to resolve this matter in an amicable manner," a Snap spokesperson said in a statement.

But there remains a federal case involving Meta, TikTok, Snap and YouTube and a litany of nationwide school districts, along with various state attorneys general suits. In August, Meta agreed to pay nearly $17 billion to settle with a group of state AGs in a child mental health case. Some AGs are still pursuing similar suits against Meta's rivals.

The social media lawsuits have helped inform parents and the public about how the industry designs products used by teenagers and children, McNally said.

"It's the technology arms race that continues," he said. "What is oftentimes missing is how you balance that participation in the arms race with utility and then product safety."

Google ran into more criticism this week for moving too fast. The New York Times reported on Tuesday that K-12 schools that don't allow use of Google's AI tools for students will lose access to search. Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that, "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

That coincided with the new lawsuit against Google centering on a teen's alleged suicidal crisis that the parents attributed to the company's Gemini AI. The plaintiff's attorneys allege that "despite Gemini's known risks, Google hastily integrated" Gemini into Chromebook computers for school children.

"We are reviewing the claims in the lawsuit and we take this very seriously," a Google spokesperson said in a statement. "We've worked extensively with medical professionals and academic experts to build features that help young people find critical support and trusted resources when they need it."

Google and its internet peers have for decades been able to rely on Section 230.

Passed in 1996, the federal legislation has protected social media companies from being sued over content posted by their users. That's why plaintiffs' attorneys and state AGs have been focused on alleged harms stemming from the design of the tech platforms as opposed to their content.

Some plaintiffs are using the same tactics against AI companies. OpenAI faces several lawsuits from state AGs and plaintiff attorneys, taking cues from the social media suits in alleging that the design of ChatGPT can pose risks to children.

The litigation could become a growing financial risk as the company plans for a potential blockbuster IPO. OpenAI competitor Anthropic, which is planning to go public this month, highlighted the issue in its prospectus, even though the Claude creator prohibits children from using its product.

"This is a clear indication that tech companies anticipate more litigation risks than they did a decade ago," said Michele Neitz, a professor at the University of San Francisco School of Law.

Blocker said that the highly personalized nature of current AI-powered chat apps makes it "a little bit unpredictable" to determine exactly what the apps will say to kids. She stressed the importance of testing and evaluating these products for children's developmental safety from the start, "not after harms start happening."

This collective litigation has helped educate the public on the potential pitfalls of poor product design choices, Blocker said. And it could be a catalyzing force for change in the tech industry.

"Certainly there's a hope that this will motivate real systemic change, both from a regulation standpoint and internal company decision-making standpoint," Blocker said.

If you are having suicidal thoughts or are in distress, contact the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 for support and assistance from a trained counselor.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.
