#2969. Nets collapse late to fall against Hawks in latest heartbreaking loss

ATLANTA — The Nets have gone from suffering beatings to suffering heartbreakers.

From getting blown out to blowing late leads.

The Nets led almost all night here except when it mattered most, at the end. They committed a 124-121 collapse before a crowd of 16,117 at State Farm Arena on Saturday.

After looking in terrible disarray while dropping their first five games, the Nets (2-7) have split the past four. But those two defeats will stick in their collective craw.

Mikel Brown Jr. looks on during the Nets' 124-121 loss to the Hawks on Nov. 7, 2026 in Atlanta. NBAE via Getty Images

They blew a fourth-quarter lead in the Garden to the reigning champion Knicks. And Saturday, they squandered a 17-point cushion and had a 114-110 edge with 1:45 to play before allowing 10 unanswered points that cost them the game.

"We were not consistent enough to do the right things. And it's not about making shots, it's about trying to do the right things. Obviously, the points off turnovers, this is a team that will pressure you, and we had 22 turnovers for 28 points. That's not acceptable. That's where you lose the game," Nets coach Jordi Fernández said.

"Our effort was not great. We didn't do a lot of things. We didn't pay attention to details. Offensively, a lot of random stuff. So I guess we were just not really focused anymore," Nolan Traore said.

"We had great looks in the first half, but in the second half, we played more random stuff, so we didn't have the same quality shots. So that might be the main thing. I don't think Atlanta did something that disturbed us, it was more just us doing random stuff."

Jalen Johnson poured in a game-high 30 points for the Hawks, who stormed back. But it was the Nets' own mistakes that let them.

Michael Porter Jr. led the Nets with 25 points and nine rebounds, while Day'Ron Sharpe had a triple-double (12 points, 13 assists, 10 boards).

Julius Randle drives on Aaron Wiggins during the Nets' road loss to the Hawks. Getty Images

Young point guards Traore and Mikel Brown Jr. both played well. The former had 19 points, four steals and finished a team-best plus-9, but the problem was when he finished. He had nine points in the first quarter, but never got off the bench in the fourth.

Brown scored 13 of his 18 points in the final period, but was a minus-10.

"Mikel had those five straight points, and I just let him roll because you cannot just keep taking them in and out," Fernández said when asked why he sat Traore. "When I was about to sub Nolan in, Mikel made two or three 3s and a midrange pullup. So I thought that was the right thing to do. Was it right? I don't know."

Fernández said the fact Brown was already slated to sit out Sunday's game in Dallas had nothing to do with the decision. He was just riding the hot hand.

Michael Porter Jr. drives to the basket during the Nets' road loss to the Hawks. Getty Images

Traore stormed out of the blocks with seven quick points to spot the Nets a 12-2 lead. And the bench helped pad it to 31-14 when Brown found Noah Clowney for a 3 with 3:57 left in the first quarter.

But shaky rebounding let the Hawks get on the offensive glass and cut that to 70-66 by the break.

It was still four at 114-110 after a Brown 3.

But that's when they allowed a 10-0 run to blow it.

Sharpe's turnover handed Onyeka Okongwu a 3, and after he missed a drive, Nickeil Alexander-Walker's free throw with 1:26 left put Atlanta up for good at 115-114.

When Porter missed a 3-pointer, Alexander-Walker raced for a loose ball and a breakaway layup. Both Sharpe and Julius Randle failed to corral rebounds and let the Hawks get second chances they cashed in on.

Johnson's and-one capped the 10-0 run with 38 seconds left and essentially ended it.

"They just started hitting shots down the stretch," Brown said. "Offensive rebounds, they were killing us all game. That was really the problem."

#2967. What midterm election blue wave rebuke of Trump means for renewable energy

As recently as 2022, many leaders in corporate America and the investment community were singing the praises of ESG — environmental, social, governance — as the foundation of stakeholder capitalism. It didn't take long for that tune to change, for understandable reasons.

President Trump didn't just make "ESG", "sustainability" and "climate change" dirty words in his "green new scam" rhetoric. The Trump administration's rollback of environmental regulations, elimination of clean energy subsidies and tax credits, defunding and halting of wind and solar projects, and embrace of the fossil fuels industry accelerated the backlash.

But renewable energy investing remains alive and well, notably among public and private funders of infrastructure projects and clean technology innovation. And following the midterm elections that gave climate-friendly Democrats control of the House and Senate, the prospects of reviving the clean energy agenda suddenly brightened.

Clean energy and solar stocks rallied the day after the election results, which "potentially reduces the risk of further political interference with renewable energy incentives and provides greater protection for the long-term economics of U.S. wind, solar and battery storage investments," Barclays analysts led by Peter Crampton wrote in a note.

While Trump can veto legislation to restore subsidies and credits and revive projects, and continue to issue executive orders on energy policy, some Republicans may be more inclined to help Democrats override the president in order to appeal to voters outraged over high energy prices — a sentiment expressed in the passage of multiple state initiatives to impede the build-out of electricity-hungry data centers.

The blue wave also shines a spotlight on a bipartisan permitting-reform bill awaiting Senate approval. Several progressive Democrats want to put off the vote until the new Congress convenes in January, giving them time to rewrite provisions favoring renewables projects over fossil fuel ventures.

Ultimately, the midterms could improve the outlook for investors focused on clean energy. That is likely to start with impact investors who specifically seek to generate a competitive financial rate of return alongside positive, proactive social and environmental impact. Opportunities can be found through public equity investments like mutual funds and ETFs that are constructed to invest in clean energy companies. But many investors also seek out pure-play clean energy positions offered through fixed income, private equity, private credit, venture capital, real assets and community-focused investments.

"There are many different approaches that could lead impact investors to be thinking about clean energy investments," said Amit Bouri, who's retiring as CEO of the Global Impact Investing Network at year's end. GIIN's 2026 survey of impact investors found that 56% have funds invested in clean energy and 25% plan to increase those investments over the next five years. The survey was conducted prior to the midterms.

"Clean energy infrastructure can be really compelling from an investment standpoint, because the financial thesis and impact thesis are mutually reinforcing," said Justina Lai, chief impact officer at LNW, a wealth management advisory firm. LNW has about $3.5 billion of its total $12.1 billion assets under management invested in impact strategies. She added that it's challenging to break down the number of clients who are specifically focused on clean energy and clean tech. "Climate change or climate action writ large is likely the most common impact priority that we encounter with our clients," she said.

While it might be hard to focus on the potential in the renewable energy sector in 2026 with energy commodities leading all sectors of the market as crude oil prices surged, some of the factors Lai cites should be on the radar of investors beyond the impact investing space.

She pointed to the nation's growing electricity demand — partly tied to data centers — its aging and constrained grid infrastructure, the increasing need for energy storage, the effects of geopolitics on energy security and rising energy costs as major factors driving clean energy investing. Simultaneously, though, because of declining costs and continuing maturity of many clean technologies, "we know that renewables are the lowest cost form of new-build generation, even on a fully unsubsidized basis," she said.

The proof is in the gigawatts. The U.S. is on pace to add a record 89 GW of clean energy capacity by the end of 2026, with installations projected to exceed that level in 2027, according to BloombergNEF's latest US Clean Energy Market Outlook. In just the first half of this year, the Environmental Defense Fund and Atlas Public Policy recently reported, 11.5 GW of solar, 8.3 GW of battery storage and 6.3 GW of wind power were installed.

U.S. investment in clean energy and transportation alone totaled $75 billion in the second quarter of 2026, the second-highest quarterly figure on record and up 22% from the previous quarter, according to the Clean Investment Monitor, a joint project of Rhodium Group and MIT's Center for Energy and Environmental Policy Research.

The supply chain has amped up, too. By the third quarter of this year, manufacturing capacity for solar modules, wind blades and nacelles had expanded by roughly 50% from the first quarter of 2025, reflecting new facilities that have come online, the CIM reported in October. And over the past four quarters, $277 billion was invested across the country in the manufacture and deployment of renewable energy, non-fossil fuel vehicles, building electrification and carbon-management technology.

"Investment has consistently been very strong in solar and storage at the utility scale," said Hannah Hess, director, energy and climate at Rhodium. That makes sense from a bottom-line perspective, she said, "particularly when some of the deadlines for these projects have changed for them to be eligible for tax credits."

Hess is referring to provisions in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which significantly hastened the sunsets and deadlines for federal clean energy tax credits compared to the original timeline set by the Biden administration's Inflation Reduction Act. Solar and wind projects had to begin construction by July 4 of this year or be placed into service by December 31, 2027, to claim standard investment or production tax credits.

Meanwhile, even with the Trump administration's concerted efforts to rescind much of the $1.6 trillion set aside for clean energy under Biden's Bipartisan Infrastructure Law and the IRA, nearly $600 billion remains available, according to a Politico analysis. Keeping that third of the funding pipeline open bodes well for clean energy developers and investors, especially considering the midterm results.

"Despite the gutting of the IRA, our long-term thesis and our clients' long-term views on clean energy remain the same," said Roraj Pradhananga, chief investment officer at Veris Wealth Partners, an advisory firm that specializes in impact investing. "If we look at the volatility and risk-adjusted returns of the traditional energy sector, in the long run, shifting to renewable energy makes the most [economic] sense," even when taking the tax credits out of the equation, he said.

Veris manages $3 billion across asset classes, Pradhananga said, using various vehicles, including public markets, private debt and infrastructure funds. "Climate solutions tend to be the largest allocation for our clients. We recently allocated to strategies from GPs like Closed Loop Partners, Streetlife Ventures, SJF Ventures, New Energy Capital and Sunwealth."

The proliferation of AI data centers was a hot-button issue in the midterms. Voters in 10 states weighed in on 41 local initiatives, which included complete bans on new construction, pausing ongoing and planned facilities and restrictions on energy consumption. All but one passed. And although newly elected governors in three states — Ohio, Texas and Georgia — ran on platforms critical of data centers, they're not calling for permanent bans.

For instance, Gina Hinojosa, governor-elect of Texas, said during a CNN interview on November 5, "We will have laws to protect Texans, and we need to stop what we are doing with data centers until we have those protections." She also emphasized giving local communities a meaningful say in whether and how facilities operate. That echoes Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro's cautious approach to data center development in that state. In August, he issued an executive order that places a host of restrictions on developers.

The upshot for AI companies may be a slowdown in new data center construction, increased input from local residents and officials, and self-financing for electricity and water usage. Yet they are not expected to change their long-term expansion plans. A few days before the election, Goldman Sachs said, "While U.S. data center development faces increasing political and community opposition, project data and our updated capacity estimates suggest that the...growth outlook through 2027 remains largely unchanged." The Wall Street firm forecast data center power demand to grow 38% or 12 GW in 2026 and 38% or 17 GW in 2027.

On Friday, OpenAI's CFO told CNBC the company will not be changing its investment plans despite the election results. White House economic advisor Kevin Hassett expressed a similarly optimistic view of continued investing on CNBC on Friday. "We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

From another perspective, the risks associated with AI and data centers could be beneficial to renewable energy investments. "If anything, sustainability concerns around AI actually reinforce their interest in climate action," Lai said. "The growth in AI and data centers could drive significant new fossil fuel generation, so our clients are motivated to ensure that more of that [electricity] demand is met by renewables."

That's true for many of Veris' clients, too, Pradhananga said. But he also acknowledged that because natural gas is a prominent fuel source for large-scale utilities, as well as behind-the-meter LNG turbines that power many data centers, "there are some [of our] clients who view this as part of the transition" from fossil fuels to renewables, espousing the often-mentioned "all of the above" approach to the changeover. "This is where we do have exposure to utilities in [our] public markets portfolio."

Public consternation over data centers, a Democratic-controlled Congress come January and, at least for now, a voting map across the country that has swung significantly towards the Democratic Party ahead of the 2028 presidential election, could create a trifecta of tailwinds for investors in renewable energy and clean-tech. And while any moratorium on data centers could impact renewable project development on a case-by-case basis, it might also promote investments in transmission grid upgrades that allow utility-scale solar and wind installations to tie into existing and proposed data centers.

It could also speed up research and development of emissions-free power generation, energy-efficiency and other technologies that climate-tech VCs are funding. Due to the AI boom, renewable energy companies have faced stiffer competition in recent years for venture capital dollars previously targeting climate change and decarbonization. But climate-tech VC hit $26 billion in the first half of 2026, a 55% increase year over year, according to market-intelligence firm Currence.

"There are very interesting infrastructure opportunities in [climate investing], including advanced fusion ventures, floating offshore wind turbines and solar plus storage operations," said Adam Wolfensohn, a clean-energy investment advocate and advisor. "All of these technologies keep getting better and cheaper, unlocking so much more potential for renewables and new financial structures. It's a rich time to be looking at this space."

Among the guiding resources the GIIN offers to its 350 member organizations in 40-plus countries, including 126 in the U.S., is a recently launched climate solutions investing framework. "It's designed to help investors make sense of the landscape of climate solutions and to ensure they have rigor around the actual climate impacts underpinning an investment strategy," Bouri said. "A lot of things have green labels on them, but what we are really focused on is what drives real results for people and the planet."

#2966. Lions and Dolphins are trading successful fake punts in Week 9

They are trading completions in South Beach between the visiting Detroit Lions and the Miami Dolphins.

And that is not limited to quarterbacks Jared Goff and Malik Willis.

Both teams have now pulled off successful fake punts in this game, with punters Jack Fox and Bradley Pinion getting in on the passing game fun.

Detroit was first to call for a fake punt, early in the second quarter. With the Lions facing a 4th-and-7 on their own 19-yard line, Dan Campbell rolled the dice. But Fox delivered a strike, throwing a near-perfect hitch route to Tay Martin along the left side of the field:

With this timing and anticipation, one almost wonders if Fox once played quarterback.

He did, throwing 18 touchdowns as a quarterback at Ladue Horton Watkins high school in Ladue, Missouri. Thanks to Ian Rapoport for dusting off that statistic.

Not to be outdone, the Dolphins dialed up a creative fake punt of their own in the second half. With Miami facing a 4th-and-3 near midfield early in the fourth quarter, Jeff Hafley rolled the dice himself, with Pinion finding a wide-open Kaytron Allen for the first down. Take note of this design, as Allen comes underneath the line and releases through the middle of the formation, making this an easy throw for the punter:

Only one of these fakes resulted in points, however. The Lions were forced to punt a few plays later after their fake, while the Dolphins turned their extended possession into a field goal.

Update: According to Scott Hanson on RedZone, this is the first game in league history where both punters completed a pass.

#2964. Knicks 109, Cavaliers 97: “ANOTHER GAME HOLDING THE BAD GUYS UNDER 100”

The Cavaliers (5-5) beat the Sixers at the Rocket Arena yesterday, so maybe you can blame their sulfurous, rotten-egg performance tonight on fatigue. In their first return to Ohio since sweeping the Eastern Conference Finals, the Knicks (5-4) picked up right where they left off. After piling on a 19-point lead in the third, our heroes let Donovan Mitchell (37 PTS, 13-22 FG) close the gap in the fourth, but he was outdueled yet again by Jalen Bruson (31 PTS, 12-25 FG), Karl-Anthony Towns (29 PTS, 13 RBS) was too strong, and the Knicks closed the door, 109-97.

Not only did our heroes knock off a rival, they played lock-down defense again. We share RJParrot's enthusiasm: "ANOTHER GAME HOLDING THE BAD GUYS UNDER 100."

Evan Mobley went right at Karl-Anthony Towns, who recorded his first foul with less than thirty seconds gone. Savvy strategy, Kenny Atkinson, but it wouldn't work tonight. After that foul, Towns did a good job of avoiding his second infraction for the rest of the half while still playing aggressively and collecting 18 points on 12 shots. He finished the game with two fouls. KAT's not just hungry for that second ring; I think he's gunning for MVP.

Josh Hart is still mending a sore ankle, so Landry Shamet started for him again. Shamet has proven to be an offensive weapon, but he attempted two shots in his 13 first-half minutes.

The Cavs had three rotation players on their injury report. With Jarrett Allen out, Meleek Thomas, the 34th pick in this year's draft, made his second start. The 20-year-old neophyte (9 PTS, 3-10 FG, 24 MIN) drilled back-to-back bombs to give his squad a 16-14 lead with five minutes left. Mike Brown used a timeout to regroup his squad and sub in Mohamed Diawara (2 PTS, 3 RBS, +16, 14 MIN) and Andre Drummond (8 RBS, 14 MIN). Soon after, Dalton Knecht (5 MIN) made his second appearance for the Knicks—and blundered by fouling Mitchell for what became a four-point play.

OAKAAKUYOAK Mario Hezonja is back in the league! He played nine minutes off the bench in the first half for the Ohio Players, scoring zero on two shots. Starter Peyton Watson (who was rumored to have a sore hammy) took six of Cleveland's first 13 shots, while Mobley had attempted none, Donovan Mitchell one, and James Harden two.

At the other end, Mike Brown was featuring Jalen Brunson and Towns. The latter made all four freebies at the line, while the former missed five of his first six shots. A slow start for Detective Madison, but stick around for sparks. Tyler Kolek also checked in to play alongside Knecht, Shamet, Anunoby, and Towns for the final two minutes. The Knicks were trailing by four, so the move was a vote of confidence by the coach.

With Towns and Anunoby smashing to the rim, the visitors were able to close the gap. The Knicks missed eight three-pointers in the opening frame, were outrebounded by three, and attempted half as many free throws as the Cavs, but nonetheless trailed by a single point at the break, 27-26. Math!

New York's awful long-range shooting persisted as they missed their first eight attempts of the second quarter. Mikal Bridges (9 PTS, 4-13 FG), in particular, had another woeful offensive half (during which he shot 0-5 from deep), but he redeemed himself with a few fine defensive efforts. Loved when he made the Beard look silly on a drive.

The ink still wet on his contract, Riley Minix provided eight points on 3-of-3 shooting off the bench tonight.

The scoreboard tilted back and forth. New York's shooting improved. Thomas checked back in for the hosts, and he drilled his third triple in Diawara's grill. The sophomore was +12 in the half, though, thanks to sharing the floor with Brunson, Jordan Clarkson, Bridges, and Drummond. Cap was cooking. He scored 18 straight Knicks points, outscoring the Cavs by seven himself and giving New York a 52–47 lead.

From there, McBride canned a jumper, Shamet drilled a dinger, and Anunoby hit consecutive triples to give the good guys a 63-53 advantage.

Through the half, both teams shot 44%, but the Knicks got 13 more shots, thanks to ten offensive boards. They also committed just three turnovers to the Cavs' eight and won the points-off-turnovers battle 12-2. The home team shot 46% from deep to New York's 32% and still trailed. Brunson topped the box score with 20 so far, while Spida led the Cavs with 11.

Cleveland opened the third with four straight points, both baskets set up by Donovan Mitchell, to cut the Knicks' lead to 63–57. Jalen Brunson then found Mikal Bridges for a three, bringing him to 9-34 from deep this season. Karl-Anthony Towns cut for a layup to stretch the lead back to 12.

Evan Mobley kept Cleveland close with six points in the opening five minutes, including back-to-back buckets off James Harden feeds. Tyrese Proctor's step-back three made it 72–65 after a Knicks possession that produced three misses, three offensive rebounds, and a shot-clock violation.

Brunson took over from there. He scored six straight on a floater, a running layup, and a fadeaway, then found Landry Shamet for a three to cap a 9–0 run. Mitchell broke it with a layup, but Brunson fed Mohamed Diawara for one of his own, sending Cleveland to timeout down 83–67. Very encouraging signs from Mo!

Towns chipped in four points, including a putback after Peyton Watson blocked his dunk attempt. OG Anunoby (15 PTS, 4-13 FG) had a block and two boards but missed all four of his shots and scored only at the line. Harden's two assists to Mobley helped briefly, but he went scoreless in the stretch. When Shamet swished his third triple of the night, New York's advantage was 19.

With the Knicks playing clean defense and the Cavs running on fumes, a 25-17 third quarter gave our heroes an 88-70 lead heading into the fourth.

The road got bumpy at the outset of the final frame. Kolek acquired his fourth foul; Shamet, Towns, and Anunoby missed multiple shots; and Mitchell and Mobley paired for eight points. Kolek's been a reliable floor general to head the second unit, but his shooting this season stands at 5-of-19 after tonight's 0-for-2. Seeing the need for heroics, Towns supplied them while Brunson sat. Smashing the boards and exploiting the false wall of Cleveland's frontcourt, he reached 29 points, logged his ninth double-double of the season, and scored five straight to restore a 15-point lead.

KAT sat at the middle mark. From there Cap and Spida traded blows. The latter scored Cleveland's next eight points, and with the score at 102-92, Mike Brown signaled for timeout with 3:15 remaining. Not ready to release his guys yet, Atkinson called for a timeout, too. Time for another Novig commercial.

Out of the break, Brunson and Mitchell traded contested jumpers. KAT was back, but Mobley boxed him out on a Brunson miss. Watson somehow corraled a slippery ball for a layup. Eight-point lead with 1:53 left.

The struggling Bridges came up with a clutch bucket. Shamet fouled Spida, who made one, missed one. Shamet missed from deep, but this time KAT got the board and pitched it to Cap, who was fouled. Just like Donovan, Jalen missed his first freebie. Mitchell tried a rushed trey with Shamet covering, which rimmed out. Anunoby grabbed the board; Spida swatted it out of bounds and got Kenny to contest it. Not only was the call confirmed, but a foul on the Cav was added to it. Insult to injury, you say? Indeed. Sweet victory!

Our heroes get two nights off before hosting the Bucks on Tuesday. Safe travels, Knickerbockers.

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2960. Jalen Brunson, Karl-Anthony Towns dominate as Knicks roll past Cavaliers again

CLEVELAND — It felt like early November on the streets of Northeast Ohio. Inside Rocket Arena, it looked like late May.

Jalen Brunson was untouchable. Karl-Anthony Towns was unstoppable. Analytics were unnecessary. The eye test was enough for anyone to determine the best team on the floor.

The Knicks extended their dominance over the Cavaliers in their first meeting since their Eastern Conference finals sweep, earning their first three-game winning streak of the season with a 109-97 win Saturday night.

After becoming the first defending champion in 15 years to drop its first three games of the season, the Knicks (5-4) are now over .500 for the first time this season.

Jalen Brunson, who scored a game-high 31 points drives past James Harden en route to the basket during the Knicks' 109-97 win over the Cavaliers on Nov. 7, 2026 in Cleveland. NBAE via Getty Images

"Going into the season … one of my concerns was, 'Are we gonna attack this season the way we should?' and obviously we didn't," head coach Mike Brown said. "That's why we got knocked in the head, and I was OK with it because right now is the time to get knocked in the head. If you have championship pedigree — which that group does have — it's going to wake you up, and it's going to make you realize that nobody's going to give anything to us, and it's harder than what we think.

"When you win games, you're human, it's easy to start letting go of the rope because you just start to relax, especially over an 82-game season. And now the challenge for us is, can we hold on to the rope and keep climbing a little higher?"

Brunson initially struggled in his return to the floor where the Knicks clinched their first NBA Finals berth in 27 years, but eventually resembled the Finals MVP, finishing with 31 points (12-for-25 shooting, including 3-for-7 on 3-pointers), nine assists and six rebounds.

Towns had 29 points, 13 rebounds and two steals.

The Cavaliers (5-5), coming off an impressive win over the 76ers the previous night, ended their back-to-back home set looking as far from contention as the team that lost the final three games of the conference finals by a combined 66 points. Donovan Mitchell led Cleveland with 37 points.

"We found a little bit of that magic and that pace of that team that was so magical in last year's run," Towns said. "Just fortunate that we were able to do something special tonight."

Karl-Anthony Towns, who scored 29 points and grabbed 13 rebounds, drives on James Harden during the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers. David Richard-Imagn Images

The foul-prone Towns was whistled on a call 22 seconds into the game, but he remained aggressive, overpowering the Cavs for 14 points and four rebounds in the first quarter.

Brown searched for other answers — throwing out 11 players as the Knicks fell behind by six and missed 16 of their first 19 3-pointers — but the coach just needed the man most responsible for ending the franchise's 53-year title drought.

Brunson made his return visit to Cleveland look like an extension of the Eastern Conference finals, scoring 18 straight points in the second quarter to swing momentum, as the Knicks went to halftime with a 63-53 lead.

Donovan Mitchell, who scored a game-high 37 points, looks on during the Knicks' road win over the Cavaliers. NBAE via Getty Images

"He's getting rhythm, but it's one of those things that you know you don't worry about a guy like him," Brown said of Brunson. "You don't worry about a guy like KAT because they're gonna find their way sooner than later. And he and KAT are both more than capable of doing what they did tonight."

Landry Shamet hit back-to-back 3-pointers late in the third quarter to give the Knicks an 88-69 lead, quieting all but the countless blue-and-orange fans in the crowd.

With James Harden invisible again — scoring seven points on 2-for-9 shooting — Mitchell tried to will the Cavs back in it by scoring 22 of the team's first 26 fourth-quarter points, bringing Cleveland within eight with under two minutes remaining.

"I think we're in a good place," Brunson said. "Obviously, bounced back from our start, but just going to try to do our best to not be complacent. Continue to get better every single day. Just because we've been winning a couple games doesn't mean like we're just we're back to who we are. We are going to continue to work. That's just our emphasis right now."

#2959. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2957. The Broncos’ fingertip interception was wild and shouldn’t have been allowed

The Denver Broncos have one of the most fearsome defenses in the NFL, not just because they hit hard, but also because they have ballhawks in the secondary. Denver entered its Week 9 matchup with the Carolina Panthers tied for seventh in the league in interceptions, and they added another one at the end of the third quarter in one of the wildest plays of the season for multiple reasons.

Carolina QB Bryce Young threw the ball into the end zone for tight end Tommy Tremble, but it was successfully defended by Denver linebacker Justin Strnad. Tremble made a second effort to catch the ball, and it never actually hit the ground, staying in the air just long enough for Broncos defensive back Devon Key to grab it with his fingertips. It sure seemed like Key picked up the ball off the turf in real time. The Panthers lined up for the next play, and snapped the ball, running it with Chuba Hubbard.

THEN the refs whistled and said they saw Payton's challenge flag before Carolina snapped the ball, and reviewed the interception by Denver. It turns out Key grabbed the ball before it hit the turf, resulting in an interception. Watch the play here:

Here's another angle of the pick that clearly shows Key grabbed the ball before it hit the ground.

Did Sean Payton really get the challenge off before the snap? That's the real question. The refs' decision to honor the challenge changed the game, because Denver went down and scored a touchdown after the interception.

The Broncos just always seem to have luck on their side in the regular season. This is a ridiculous pick by Key either way.

#2956. Will this week's blue wave election usher in a Democratic president in 2028?

In surfer slang, a wave that lasts a long time is a "runner." A key question for investors is whether the blue wave that washed over the country Tuesday was that kind of wave, and could it ferry Democrats to victory in 2028.

Statistically, the answer is it should have little effect.

CNBC analyzed elections since 1938 and looked at the biggest midterm wave elections where the party in the White House lost seats to determine whether they portend anything about the presidential election two years later.

Tuesday's election was the biggest House seat loss for the incumbent party in the White House since 2018, but it only barely makes the historical Top 10. It ranks 10th in CNBC's analysis using an estimate of 41 House seats gained by the opposition party as close races are still being tallied. The largest loss came in 1938 when Democrats lost 72 House seats amid a renewed recession, opposition to court-packing by President Franklin Delano Roosevelt and the president's battle with conservatives in his own party. Roosevelt went on to win reelection two years later in 1940.

In more modern times, Republicans swept to power in the House in 2010 two years into Barack Obama's first presidential term, with Democrats losing 64 seats, though retaining control of the Senate. Obama went on to reelection two years later. Of the top four wave elections, which all occurred before a president's final term, the incumbent went on to win reelection each time.

But for the next five waves, the incumbent party lost, including John F. Kennedy beating Richard Nixon in 1960 after Republicans lost seats under President Dwight Eisenhower in 1958; and Joe Biden beating Donald Trump in 2020 after Republicans lost seats in 2018.

Jay Campbell, partner at Hart Associates, the Democratic pollster for the CNBC All-America Economic Survey, said the reason midterm waves may not carry forward is the importance of individual characters in presidential elections.

"A lot can happen in two years. And while presidential elections are certainly subject to the overall political environment, they are also very much about each party's nominee," Campbell said. "If candidate quality matters in congressional elections, it matters 1,000 times more in presidential ones."

It's a long way to 2028 and this wave, gnarly as it may have been, could statistically peter out just as easily as it could wash Democrats back into the White House.

#2955. Hawks' Jalen Johnson scores 30 in comeback from injury

Jalen Johnson scored 30 points in his return from an ankle injury and the Atlanta Hawks beat the Brooklyn Nets 124-121 on Saturday, Nov. 7 at State Farm Arena. The Hawks were down by as many as 17 points in the first quarter.

Johnson, 24, had 12 points points in the fourth quarter to bring his team back on top for good. With 39 seconds left in the game, he raced past Julius Randle and drove to the basket to toss in a floater. He was fouled and made the free throw to help secure the victory. The forward also had seven rebounds and six assists.

Nickeil Alexander-Walker and Aaron Wiggins added 20 points for Atlanta in the Hawks' fourth straight win. Michael Porter Jr. had 25 points for Brooklyn.

Johnson hadn't played in a week, missing three games after spraining his left ankle in the Hawks' loss to the New Orleans Pelicans on Halloween.

The day of the Nets matchup, he was listed as probable on the injury report and then was upgraded to available shortly before tipoff.

The Hawks took Johnson as the 20th overall pick in the 2021 NBA Draft. He earned his first All-Star nod last season when he 22.5 points, 10.3 rebounds and 7.9 assists per game.

He is averaging 18.3 points, 9.0 boards and 7.9 assists this season.

#2952. Trump says U.S. carried out ‘minor strikes’ on Iran as missiles hit Qeshm Island

President Donald Trump told reporters Friday that the U.S. carried out "minor strikes" on Iran, amid reports that two missiles struck Qeshm Island near the Strait of Hormuz.

The missiles struck the island on Friday, Iranian news agency Tasnim reported, according to Reuters. Tasnim said there was no official information on damage, Reuters added.

The island lies off Iran's southern coast, near the Strait of Hormuz, the vital artery for oil and gas that has become a flashpoint in the war.

Speaking to reporters Friday evening after landing in West Palm Beach, Florida, Trump didn't comment specifically on Qeshm Island, saying: "I don't want to talk about it."

But he added: "We do minor strikes, but they knock out. They built a little bit of an anti-aircraft device, and we knocked it out in about 15 minutes, maybe five." He did not explicitly confirm that he was referring to the reported strikes on Qeshm.

The strikes are the first since Tuesday's midterm elections, after analysts and regional officials had debated whether the vote would lead Trump toward further escalation or renewed diplomacy.

The U.S. struck the same island Monday, in what a U.S. official told MS Now was a "defensive measure."

Trump also told reporters Friday that Iran wants "to make a deal very badly. They have 300% inflation. Their navy's gone. Their airport, everything's gone, and they want to make a deal."

The International Monetary Fund forecast that Iran's consumer prices would rise by an average of 78.6% in 2026, in a report published Oct. 13.

When asked if he wanted to make a deal "really soon," Trump said: "Well, we have nobody getting hurt, nobody getting killed.

"You know, this is a war that we may have been there for seven months, but we probably fought for three or four weeks, really fought. And again, you look at World War One; that's a war. World War Two; that's a war."

Iran has said it wants more than "paper commitments" from the U.S. before entering a new phase of diplomatic talks.

"We must see practical measures by the Americans," Ali Bagheri Kani, deputy secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, told state broadcaster IRIB on Friday, according to Press TV.

#2949. Berkshire shares surge as Wall Street swoons amid AI fears

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway shares roared past the S&P 500 index this week as investors moved money away from slumping tech stocks and moved into what appears to be the relative safety of a traditional conglomerate with relatively little exposure to artificial intelligence amid fears massive spending on data centers won't pay off.

One month ago, Berkshire's widely held B shares were underperforming the S&P by 13.6 percentage points for the year.

With help from three more record-high closes this week, Berkshire is now 5.8 percentage points ahead of the benchmark.

Berkshire more than doubled its share repurchases last quarter to $9.6 billion, while operating earnings declined slightly, the company said on Saturday.

Berkshire was up more than 5% this week while the S&P fell almost 2.7%.

The contrast is even sharper with the Nasdaq Composite, which fell 3.4% on the week.

Thursday's especially heavy tech selling pushed the index down more than 10% below its most recent high in what is commonly referred to as a "correction," but it did make up some of its lost ground Friday.

Two weeks ago, Berkshire's share prices completely erased all of the roughly 15% drop they suffered in the three months following Warren Buffett's surprise announcement at the 2025 shareholders meeting that he would step down as CEO at the end of that year.

Now they are up 6% from the record high closes they hit the Friday before Buffett's revelation on May 3, 2025.

Berkshire is not completely removed from the convulsions around AI.

Abel told us in early September he sees a "significant opportunity" ahead for Berkshire Hathaway Energy to provide electricity to data centers.

He also expected Berkshire would benefit from its big bet on Google parent Alphabet, saying he and Warren Buffett knew AI was "going to have a significant impact on America and businesses," and "we saw Google as a significant player."

Now that investment is getting negative reviews this week as the stock slumps along with other companies related to AI.

As of the end of June, Berkshire reported it owned almost 106 million shares of Alphabet, including 28.6 million shares it bought directly from the company in June: $5 billion of its Class A shares (GOOGL) for $351.81 each and another $5 billion of Class C shares (GOOG) for $348.20 each.

It bought another $7 billion of Alphabet shares on the open market during the second quarter, adding to the position it started building in last year's third quarter, an investment initiated by Warren Buffett.

Bloomberg suggests that "with cracks in the AI trade starting to show, Berkshire's sudden push [into AI] may appear ill-timed."

On June 30, the position had a market value of $37.8 billion and it was the third largest equity holding in Berkshire's portfolio.

Since then both the Class A and Class C shares have dropped by around 20% and the stake is now valued at $30.2 billion, falling behind Coca-Cola's $38.3 billion to the portfolio's #4 position.

(In just over a week, we'll find out whether Berkshire made any changes to its Alphabet position during the third quarter ending September 30.)

Bloomberg quotes CFRA Research analyst Cathy Seifert as saying, "I do think that part of this may be a conscious decision to increase their exposure. One has to ask the question though, is it a little late?"

But the article notes that "for dedicated value investors and longtime Berkshire observers, such jitters may not really matter," with Keefe Bruyette & Woods analyst Meyer Shields saying Abel probably sees bets on AI as a long-term wager on the technology's potential, in keeping with the "conservatism that you would expect at Berkshire Hathaway."

At Barron's, however, Andrew Bary sees what he estimates is a $3 billion loss on Alphabet as part of a "disappointing" long-term trend in Berkshire's stock choices

"The company hasn't created much value from stock picking during the current decade" even though the "S&P 500 has returned 150% since the end of 2019 and tech stocks have quadrupled."

While Berkshire's investments in five Japanese "trading houses," Chevron, and Chubb have done well, "there have been plenty of losers or simply mediocre investments that have been closed out."

He cites Verizon, HP, Paramount (now Skydance), General Motors, Constellation Brands, and Pool.

While he estimates Berkshire's Occidental Petroleum investment is up about 20% from what it paid, OXY is "far behind most energy stocks" amid this year's big increase in crude prices.

Bary also lists stocks that have continued to rise in price after Berkshire cut its stakes, with Apple being the worst offender.

That stake has been reduced by 75% since 2023 at what Bary calculates is an average selling price around $200 per share, well below AAPL's current $342.

Despite those sales, Apple is Berkshire's largest equity holding with a market value of $78 billion. The stock is up 1200% since Berkshire started buying it at the beginning of 2016, at a time when many thought the company had already seen its best days.

Bary thinks Abel will probably keep Berkshire's core equity investments but may concentrate more on his strengths going forward: improving Berkshire's operating companies and making new acquisitions.

His conclusion: "Barring a market rout, equity investing may not be central to Berkshire anymore.

"And if any new Berkshire holdings are revealed soon, it may not be a good idea to buy them. The record hasn't been great."

Berkshire Hathaway's disclosure that it had made a relatively small $27 million addition to its $4.1 billion Sirius XM position helped fuel an ongoing rally that lifted the satellite radio company's shares to a 52-week high this week.

The stock ended 4.9% higher that day at $28.67.

On Friday, Monday, and Tuesday, Berkshire bought more than 924 thousand shares at an average price of $29.10 as the stock continued to gain ground, ending Tuesday at $30.80.

Berkshire disclosed the buys late Tuesday evening, apparently helping to fuel an additional 8.4% gain as the stock closed at $33.40 on Wednesday.

It hit a 52-week intraday high of $33.76 on Thursday but finished lower Friday at $32.40.

Overall, Sirius XM is up 18.6% since the earnings release and has gained 62.0% so far this year.

It's the first addition to Berkshire's stake since late-July and early-August last year.

In 2025, it bought a total of 7.3 million shares at an average price of $21.86.

The position is likely one of portfolio manager Ted Weschler's stocks.

Pacific Power, a unit of Berkshire Hathaway's PacifiCorp, is asking California regulators for a 57% rate increase that would raise the typical residential electric bill by $120 a month.

The Wall Street Journal calls it a "stunning request even in an era of rising electricity costs."

The utility says the hike would be partially offset by a 14% decrease taking effect on January 1.

A lot of the $103 million that would be generated by the increase is already being spent on efforts to make power equipment in the sparsely populated and heavily wooded area more resistant to wildfires.

The utility is facing around $637 million in liability claims for fires in 2020 and 2022.

This week, Reuters reports, Oregon's Supreme Court heard arguments on whether to revive a jury verdict that could expose PacifiCorp "to tens of billions of dollars of potential damages over its role in four Oregon wildfires in 2020."

Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger share their opinions about how artificial intelligence could affect the economy, the government, and how much time people spend on leisure activities.

ANDREW ROSS SORKIN: Thomas Kimay (PH) is here. He's a 27-year-old shareholder from Kentfield, California.

And I should preface this question by saying that he was here 17 years ago at 10 years old, asked you a question from the audience asking you if the internet might hurt some of Berkshire's investments.

At the time, you said you wanted to see how things would play out. He's now updated the question. (Buffett laughs)

"What do you think about the implications of artificial intelligence on Berkshire's businesses, beyond autonomous driving and GEICO, which you've talked about already? ...

WARREN BUFFETT: I certainly have no special insights on artificial intelligence, but I will bet a lot of things happen in that field in the next couple of decades, and probably a shorter timeframe.

They should lead, I would certainly think — but again, I don't bring much to this party. But I would certainly think they would result in significantly less employment in certain areas.

And it may not be good for a given business, but let's take it to the extreme.

Let's assume one person could push a button and, essentially, through various machines and robotics, all kinds of things, turn out all of the output we have in this country.

So, everybody's — there's just as much output as we have. It's all being done by, you know, instead of 150-some million people being employed, one person.

You know, is the world better off or not?

Well, certainly we'd work a lot less hours a week — of work per week and so on.

I mean, it would be a good thing, but it would require enormous transformation in how people relate to each other, what they expect of government, you know, all kinds of things. And, of course, as a practical matter, more than one person would keep working.

But pushing the idea that way is one of the — you'd certainly think that's one of the consequences of making great progress in artificial intelligence.

And that's enormously prosocial, eventually. It's enormously disruptive in other ways. And it can have huge problems, in terms of a democracy and how it reacts to that...

CHARLIE MUNGER: Well, you're painting a very funny world where everybody's engaged in trade. And the trade is, I give you golf lessons and you dye my hair. And that would be a world kind of like the royal family of Kuwait or something.

And I don't think it would be good for America to have everything produced by one person and the rest of us just engaged in leisure.

#2945. America doesn't like socialism. So why are socialists winning?

Democratic socialists just had their biggest election night yet.

There's just one complication: Most American voters don't actually like socialism.

Six candidates backed by the Democratic Socialists of America won House seats Tuesday, giving the movement its largest foothold yet in Congress and greater clout in a Democratic Party already divided over how far left to go.

The victories, coming on the heels of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's rise to power, mark another milestone for a movement that has spent years challenging the party establishment. Now, with Democrats sweeping control of Congress, the question is whether those electoral gains will translate into actual political power.

The results of the new CNBC Voter Poll show the potential hazard of handing too much power to the party's left flank. The survey of roughly 80,000 midterm voters found that 53% view socialism unfavorably, compared with 33% who viewed it favorably. Capitalism fared considerably better, with 56% expressing a positive view. Just 18% of voters identified themselves as democratic socialists.

That disconnect underscores both the opportunity and the challenge facing Democrats and their growing left flank. How the party reconciles the divide between reforming capitalism and fundamentally overhauling it could shape its legislative agenda in Congress, its economic message to voters and, ultimately, its direction heading into the 2028 presidential election.

The DSA's presence in Congress will be quadrupled when Tuesday's victors, all of whom won in safely Democratic districts, are sworn in.

But the projected Democratic landslide of more than 250 House seats could dilute their influence. Party leaders could afford to lose all eight DSA votes on most measures, leaving the group with far less bargaining power than it would wield in a narrowly controlled chamber.

Still, the movement is building financial muscle. While the DSA relies on membership dues, wealthy donors are now backing its candidates through outside groups. The 12 largest donors to American Priorities, a super PAC supporting some DSA-backed candidates, gave nearly $5.9 million this cycle, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings through Oct. 31.

"We've had success with more donors but also we have an even stronger ground game to spread our message," said Megan Romer, co-chair of the DSA. "Mailers and ads alone just don't do it anymore. You have to knock on more doors and make more phone calls and talk to more people at work and in your union."

The more revealing tests on Tuesday came outside of liberal enclaves.

In Florida, DSA member Angie Nixon came within three percentage points of an upset in a state where Democrats haven't won a Senate race since 2012 and President Donald Trump won by 13 percentage points in 2024. But she lost by a wider margin than Democratic gubernatorial candidate David Jolly, who took a more moderate approach.

Michigan told a similar story. Progressive Abdul El-Sayed – backed by self-described democratic socialist Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., – won his Senate seat by seven percentage points. Yet he lagged nearly four percentage points behind Democratic Gov.-elect Jocelyn Benson's winning margin.

El-Sayed shares many DSA priorities but neither belongs to the group nor received its endorsement. He says he favors reforming capitalism over replacing it.

State ballot initiatives offered an equally mixed ideological verdict: Nearly two-thirds of voters in ruby-red Montana backed limits on corporate political spending, while deep-blue California rejected a billionaire wealth tax by roughly 10 percentage points, even as voters approved higher income taxes on the wealthy.

The results sharpen the dilemma over Democrats' identity heading into 2028, as Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., the DSA's most prominent standard-bearer, seemingly weighs a White House bid: Can left-wing populism expand the party's coalition without alienating the swing-state moderates it needs to win a presidential election – especially in a less favorable political environment?

"I think a lot of people did vote for Democrats who would not ordinarily and who probably did it ... with their teeth gritted," Romer told CNBC.

But it's clear that even voters who reject socialism aren't satisfied with capitalism as it stands.

Three out of every four participants in the CNBC Voter Poll said the condition of the U.S. economy was poor or not so good, compared with just 2% who described it as excellent. And 70% said the economic system favors the wealthy rather than treating most Americans fairly.

That frustration helped fuel a populist message far beyond the socialist left, with victorious Democrats from Adam Hamilton in Kansas to James Talarico in Texas tapping into anger over rising costs, economic inequality and the political influence of wealthy elites. Eighteen Sanders-endorsed candidates also won, including in traditionally right-leaning states like Ohio and Montana.

"The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom," Talarico, who flipped a Senate seat for Democrats in the Lone Star state, said in his victory speech. "The people at the top work so hard to keep us angry and divided because our unity is a threat to their wealth and their power."

Even politicians who weren't on the ballot took note. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a video posted to X Friday that "Democrats won by being populist economic fighters."

Economists have pointed to measures showing increasing wealth concentration and a shrinking share of the U.S. GDP heading to workers in the form of pay as signs that the divide between the country's rich and poor is widening. Coming out of the pandemic, the "K"-shaped economy has become a common descriptor for the unequal recovery seen by income classes.

Americans displayed their economic dissatisfaction well before they headed to the polls this week. The University of Michigan's closely followed consumer sentiment index hit all-time lows this year.

"We have a rising tide that is not equally lifting all boats," said Joe Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM, a tax firm. "That's not what we used to tell ourselves when we were young."

Rather than signaling a distaste for capitalism as a whole, Brusuelas said this week's results show voters want a system that also includes populist policies like Medicare for all. Or, as he calls it, "capitalism with a human face."

"These things are not mutually exclusive," said Brusuelas, who regularly briefs members of Congress on policy issues. "We can walk and chew gum at the same time."

Tuesday's Democratic sweep left some on Wall Street racing to prepare their clients' portfolios for potential policy shifts. Bank of America analysts detailed what heightened scrutiny of social media platforms or artificial intelligence data centers could mean for the shares of companies in those industries. CIBC built a basket of stocks that should benefit from a Democratically controlled Congress.

Still, the reaction of Wall Street veterans and New York City business moguls to this week's election was relatively subdued compared with Mamdani's victory in the mayoral race last year.

After Mamdani clinched his party's nomination, Pershing Square CEO Bill Ackman said he was "a bit depressed." Ahead of Mamdani's victory, Kathryn Wylde, who leads the Partnership for New York City, a nonprofit group of business executives, told CNBC that "terror is the feeling" as he rose in the polls.

Wall Street magnates wondered how a city known as the epicenter of financial markets and global capitalism could survive under a self-described socialist. Some questioned if billionaires would flee the city or whether corporations would move their headquarters — fears that largely haven't materialized since Mamdani took office.

With roughly 10 months of Mamdani's term in the rearview mirror, Anthony Chan, the former chief economist at JPMorgan, said finance moguls have realized that politics don't necessarily result in material policy changes. Chan said it's a message they've carried with them exiting this week, with many understanding that any incoming lawmakers in Washington aligned with democratic socialist policies would be a small minority.

"Many of the people that I meet that were very adamantly against and opposed to his candidacy now are sending their kids to his free daycare centers," Chan said, referencing Mamdani's partnership with the state to expand no-cost programs for 2-year-olds in New York City.

"I thought you felt that the world was going to end if this mayor came in," said Chan, who's also an alumnus of Barclays and the New York Federal Reserve. "Now, you're sending your kids to his free daycare centers."

But the DSA has even bigger ambitions for 2028. With growing political clout, infrastructure and financial backing, the movement is looking to expand its influence nationwide, while one of its most prominent allies, Ocasio-Cortez, weighs a White House run.

"That infrastructure is there, and that will make a big difference for a potential left presidential candidate," Romer said. "It's really kind of do or die time. We really have to deliver."

#2944. Nets at Mavericks Preview

The Mavericks finally got back on track Friday night with a 112-100 win over the Utah Jazz, and now they get another very winnable game Sunday against the Brooklyn Nets. Dallas enters at 5-4 after snapping a two-game losing streak, while Brooklyn entered the weekend at 2-6 and has to come to Dallas on the second night of a back-to-back after playing Atlanta on Saturday.

This is the type of game Dallas needs to start taking care of consistently. The Mavericks have already shown they can hang with much better teams, but losses like the one to Sacramento have also shown how quickly things can go sideways when they play down to an opponent. Brooklyn has enough legitimate NBA talent to make that happen again if Dallas comes out flat.

The Jazz win wasn't perfect, but there were plenty of encouraging signs.

Cooper Flagg finished with 21 points, 10 rebounds, and seven assists despite another slow start; P.J. Washington carried the offense early with 26 points, and Morez Johnson Jr. was fantastic with 13 points and 17 rebounds, including 10 on the offensive glass. Santi Aldama also made his season debut, giving Dallas eight points and nine rebounds in just 13 minutes.

That is the kind of collective production Dallas needs. Kyrie Irving and Flagg combined to shoot just 3-of-17 in the first half against Utah, but the Mavericks survived until both eventually got going. Dallas has spent too much of the early season needing Flagg or Kyrie to solve everything. Friday was a good example of what this roster can look like when Washington, Morez and the rest of the rotation actually give them something.

Brooklyn should give Dallas another opportunity to build on that.

Morez Johnson Jr. might be forcing Dusty May's hand.

The rookie was one of Dallas' best players Friday, dominating the offensive glass and giving the Mavericks the type of physicality they have badly needed with Dereck Lively II still sidelined. His 17-rebound performance was easily one of the biggest reasons Dallas controlled the second half against Utah.

Brooklyn has size with Julius Randle, Michael Porter Jr., Day'Ron Sharpe and Noah Clowney, so this will not be an easy matchup on the glass. But Morez has earned another real opportunity. Dallas has struggled with rebounding far too often this season, and right now the rookie is giving May something that is hard to take off the floor.

Aldama's return only makes the frontcourt rotation more interesting.

The Nets are young, but their two best players are not.

Michael Porter Jr. entered the weekend averaging 21.4 points and 7.4 rebounds, while Julius Randle was averaging 21 points, 7.1 rebounds, and 4.8 assists. Porter is still one of the league's more dangerous shooters when he gets comfortable, and Randle can punish smaller defenders if Dallas lets him establish position and work downhill.

That should make Washington especially important again. Dallas needs someone who can handle Randle physically without constantly forcing help, while the rest of the defense stays attached to Porter.

The Nets aren't good enough for Dallas to treat this like some impossible matchup, but they have enough individual scoring talent to make the Mavericks pay for another lazy defensive night.

This is really what Sunday comes down to.

Dallas just started a three-game homestand with a win over Utah and gets Brooklyn before Indiana comes to town Monday. The Mavericks do not need anything spectacular here. They need to defend, rebound and avoid spending the first half trying to wake themselves up.

Brooklyn will also be playing its second game in as many nights, while Dallas has Saturday off. The Mavericks have no reason to be the team lacking energy.

Dallas has already played enough difficult games to start the season. Against teams like Brooklyn, the goal should be simple: don't make the game harder than it needs to be.

When: Sunday, November 8, 6:30 p.m. CTWhere: American Airlines Center, Dallas, Texas

As of Saturday evening, Dereck Lively II, Sergio de Larrea and Tarik Biberovic are listed out for Dallas, while Caleb Martin is available. Brooklyn had not yet submitted its official injury report for Sunday's game at the time of the latest NBA report.

#2940. Trump targets Slotkin in call for DOJ probe of alleged Democratic threats to Republicans

President Donald Trump said Saturday that he was ordering the Justice Department to investigate Democratic leaders over what he described as threats against Republican officials, donors and supporters, without identifying the alleged communications or providing evidence of criminal conduct.

"The Democrats are going around threatening a lot of people, including the Attorney General of the United States, and others, with Impeachment and many other forms of punishment, even though they admit they have no reason to do so," Trump said in a Truth Social post.

The alleged threats, the president said, are "extortion at the highest level," he wrote. "I am ordering the Department of Justice to look into this criminal act, immediately!"

In his post, Trump claimed that Democratic Sen. Elissa Slotkin of Michigan is planning to "go after all young people that believe in Republican Values."

Trump was apparently referencing a speech Slotkin made at a University of Chicago event on Oct. 13. At the time, Slotkin said: "I want young people coming out of school to say, 'Should I go work for the Trump administration?'...I want them to be like, 'Lord, that might be buying myself a ton of problems that I don't want. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Slotkin alleges that she, her family and her staff faced physical and legal intimidation after the posting of a November 2025 video in which Slotkin and other Democratic leaders called on the U.S. military to "refuse illegal orders" following the airstrikes targeting boats in the Caribbean.

On Nov. 20, Trump called the video treason and said "an example must be set."

The FBI contacted lawmakers, including Slotkin, days later. In February, Slotkin declined to sit for an interview the Justice Department requested about the video.

"The intimidation is the point, and I'm not gonna go along with that," the senator said in February. A federal grand jury later declined to indict the video participants.

Now, Trump alleges Democrats are threatening legal intimidation against Republican leaders and their supporters.

"Republicans should get smart, fast! Threatening letters have been sent to many people, all innocent, from Democrat Political Leaders," the president wrote.

The White House and Slotkin didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

#2935. Stephen Curry drops 41 points vs. Magic as Warriors win seventh straight in OT

The Golden State Warriors began their season with the first 0-3 start of Stephen Curry's career. They are now 7-3.

Curry led the Warriors to their seventh straight win on Saturday, dropping 41 points to beat the Orlando Magic 126-119 in overtime. Golden State now sits in a tie for fifth in a competitive Western Conference.

In addition to his 41 points, Curry posted 6 rebounds and 6 assists while shooting 13-of-19 from the field (5-of-10 from 3-point range). Several of those points came at an inconvenient time for Orlando.

With a 10-for-10 performance at the free throw line, Curry is also now 66-of-67 from the charity stripe in eight games this season.

In addition to Curry, Yaxel Lendeborg continued a strong rookie season with 23 points, 8 rebounds, 3 assists, 3 blocks and 6 turnovers.

The game began as a one-sided affair for the Warriors, who led by 16 at halftime only for a third-quarter run to give the Magic the lead. The rest of the game was close, with Orlando forward Jamal Cain making a putback lay-up to tie the game with two seconds left.

Curry and Lendeborg combined to score the Warriors' final 12 points to put the game away in overtime.

It was easy to feel like the Warriors were in a bad place for the long term at the start of the season. Jimmy Butler is likely out until January. It's unclear when Kristaps Porzingis will play basketball again. Moses Moody is also weeks away from a return.

You don't need to remind the Warriors of where the vibes were then, as Curry mentioned after Saturday's game:

"We don't forget where people thought we were at the beginning of the year."

That team now has the second-longest winning streak in the NBA, behind only the San Antonio Spurs' eight straight victories. Next up is a home game against the New Orleans Pelicans on Monday.

#2932. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after U.S. Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether the administration still has support to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline-driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Late Saturday, U.S. Central Command said it disabled a St. Kitts-flagged ship after the tanker ignored multiple warnings and attempted to leave the region after visiting an Iranian port, according to media reports. Iran state media also reported on explosions on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, as well as several blasts occurring in the port of Jask. CNBC couldn't independently confirm the reports.

Several oil tankers have also come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, traded above $97 on Friday, while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump no longer faces future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution anyway.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11, and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

#2928. Lions' defense shuts down Malik Willis and the Dolphins in 19-9 win

The Lions' defense was playing with heavy hearts today in Miami, knowing that their leader Jack Campbell is still in a Detroit hospital after suffering a spinal injury a week ago. But the Lions heeded the call from head coach Dan Campbell to play in a way that would honor Jack Campbell.

On a day when Detroit's offense was not at its best, the defense stepped up, holding Malik Willis & Co. in check in a 19-9 win.

Derrick Moore sacked Willis twice, Alim McNeil sacked him once, and Aidan Hutchinson forced the Dolphins to hold him multiple times as Willis was under pressure all day. Willis finished 13-for-25 for 128 yards, with no touchdowns and one interception, and he managed only seven rushing yards.

Lions quarterback Jared Goff could manage only 170 yards on 17-for-25 passing, but that was enough on a day when the Lions' defense played so well.

Both punters completed passes for first downs on fake punts, and they were two of the best passes of the game on a day when neither Willis nor Goff played well.

The win improves the Lions' record to 6-2, and helps them keep pace in the very competitive NFC North. The Dolphins fall to 1-7 and keep pace in their quest for the first overall pick in the 2027 NFL draft.
